1. The vision

Glenn's original framing: “What if we could train highly-specialized small Neuro Mods to enhance generalized models that the community produces? Small, fast, transportable. Train a math expert, a coding expert, a dialog expert as separate brain files, then add the relevant ones to any base model at inference time.”

This vision has several layers of differentiation if it works:

  • Tiny size: a 92M-model brain is 16 MB; a 1.1B-model brain would still be only ~100 MB. Versus a full fine-tuned model at 4-8 GB.
  • No gradients: brain mods are built by “exposing” a frozen base model to domain content. No fine-tuning compute, no GPU training cluster.
  • Composable by addition: two brain mods are merged with M_AB = M_A + M_B. One line of tensor math, at inference, instead of LoRA-merging or model-souping rituals.
  • Unique to our architecture: no transformer-based model can do this in the same way, because transformer working memory isn't a portable tensor.

2. Proof of concept (Blog #5)

June 19, 2026.

Before chasing the product version, we needed to confirm that brain composition works at all with our 92M model. The Blog #5 experiment used synthetic concept sets: 20 random (cue, target) token pairs in disjoint vocabulary regions for set A, another 20 for set B. Built brain_A, brain_B, summed them.

Brain state A target rank B target rank Reading
baseline (M=0)10,8919,476no memory
random M10,9089,363random ≈ chance
brain A only5,505 ↓50%8,955specific to A
brain B only8,8535,495 ↓42%specific to B
brain A + B (sum)6,910 ↓37%6,380 ↓33%composition works
Milestone: brain composition is a real architectural property at 92M scale. The composed brain reduces target rank for cues from both sets, with a modest ~25% interference cost.

3. The product question

Blog #5 proved composition with synthetic concept pairs. The product vision needs it to work with real data. Two harder questions:

  1. Can a brain capture patterns from millions of tokens of real text? The trained-time decay (λ=0.98) is short — M only retains ~50 tokens of useful history. For domain specialization we'd want M to integrate over hundreds of millions of tokens.
  2. Does a brain capture domain-specific patterns? If exposing the model to math content gives a brain that helps math perplexity more than prose perplexity, we have a real product. If it just shifts perplexity uniformly, we have a curiosity.

Three candidate mechanisms to test for #1 (from a planning conversation 2026-06-19 evening):

  • Slow-decay exposure: use λ→1.0 only at exposure time. No retraining.
  • Long-decay retraining: bake longer M-horizon into the base weights from scratch.
  • Hybrid CPT + brain: short gradient-based fine-tuning + a brain snapshot.

4. Phase 1a: slow-decay exposure test

June 20, 2026.

Setup: load the phase3_5090 92M checkpoint (frozen). For each λ in {0.98, 0.999, 1.0}, expose to 100K tokens of training data (no gradients, state carried across blocks). Measure val_ppl WITH the resulting brain loaded vs WITH M=0.

λ val_ppl Δ vs M=0 max|M| Reading
baseline (M=0)69.58—0—
0.98 (native)65.62+5.69%3,387brain HELPS
0.999130.41−87%55,055catastrophic
1.0197.16−183%5,254,261totally broken
Surprise positive result: the “long-decay” strategy was supposed to be the easy path to long-term memory. It failed catastrophically. But the native λ=0.98 — the one we were going to dismiss — turned out to capture meaningful structure: brain exposure with the trained-default decay gives a clean 5.7% perplexity improvement on same-distribution data.
Honest setback (long-decay path): slowing decay to λ→1.0 breaks the model. M values explode (max|M| = 5.2 million at λ=1.0), and the trained weights weren't taught to read such large M entries. Validation perplexity goes from 70 to 197. The long-decay strategy is dead without retraining the base model.

What this means for the product: we don't need long-decay. We just need to find out whether the 5.7% improvement at native λ generalizes to specific domains. If yes, brain mods are a viable specialization mechanism out of the box.

5. Phase 1b: domain-transfer specificity (running)

June 20, 2026.

Setup: the harder question. Expose the same frozen 92M model to two disjoint chunks of the corpus (we use the validation tail split in half as a proxy for domains — no separate corpora needed). Build brain_A from chunk A, brain_B from chunk B. Measure perplexity on held-out test slices of each chunk with: no brain, brain_A, brain_B.

Success criterion: brain_A helps test_A more than test_B, and vice versa. If so, brains capture local domain patterns specifically — and the product vision is alive. If brains help both chunks equally, brains are doing generic in-context regularization, not specialization.

Setup: exposure = 200K tokens per brain (chunk A and chunk B), evaluation = perplexity over 40 held-out sequences from each chunk.

chunk no brain brain_A brain_B A help B help
A_test105.13121.69118.39−15.75%−12.61%
B_test93.06107.08106.51−15.07%−14.45%

Specificity (positive = brain helps its own domain more):

  • brain_A: −0.68% (helps A vs B)
  • brain_B: −1.84% (helps B vs A)
Setback: both brains hurt both test chunks (~15% worse perplexity), and there's no domain specificity. The product hypothesis — “exposure to domain X gives a brain that helps domain X specifically” — is not confirmed at 200K-token exposure with native λ.

5b. Phase 1b: longer-exposure brain at native λ (1M tokens)

June 20, 2026 (afternoon).

To check whether the morning's +5.7% gain at 100K tokens was real or a fluke, we extended exposure to 1M tokens at λ=0.98.

exposure tokens val_ppl Δ vs M=0 max|M| Reading
(baseline M=0)64.46—0—
100K (morning)65.62+5.69%3,387brain helps
1M (afternoon)74.40−15.43%3,209brain hurts
Critical correction: the morning's +5.7% gain reverses by 1M tokens. The brain-mod at native λ=0.98 has a narrow operating window (probably <100K tokens of exposure). Beyond that, M drifts off-distribution from what the model was trained to read, and perplexity gets worse. The 200K-token domain test was already past the sweet spot — which probably explains the −15% across the board there too.

6. What this all means (revised picture)

The afternoon's data forces an honest revision of the morning's hopeful read:

Use case Status
Brain as short-context specialization (<100K tokens exposure)probably works
Brain as million-token domain storage (the product vision)dead at current architecture
Brain composition for tightly-defined concept sets (Blog #5)still works
Slow-decay (λ→1.0) for long memorydead — M explodes

So the product story isn't dead, but it's smaller than we'd hoped. Two strongest remaining paths:

  1. Hybrid CPT + brain (Phase 1c). Short gradient-based fine-tuning of the base weights on a domain (hours, not days), plus a brain snapshot at the end. Ship as a small “kit” — weight delta in LoRA-format + brain file. The weights carry the long-term specialization; the brain carries the most-recent context.
  2. Architectural retraining. Train a new base model from scratch with a longer-decay M update rule baked in (or a normalized Hebbian rule that doesn't saturate). This is a multi-week commitment; only justified if brain mods become the central product differentiator.

7. Adjacent wins from the same run

June 20, 2026 (afternoon).

Two non-brain-mod experiments ran on the same 5080 slot and produced useful, deployable results:

Heavy sparsity penalty fix

Same 9M architecture as the baseline, with sparsity_lambda: 2.0 and sparsity_target: 0.04 (vs the default 0.5 / 0.05). Result: spike rate dropped to 4.4% (very close to the published 96% silent target) with val_ppl 167.76 vs noKD's 168.93 — essentially neutral on quality. The heavy-sparsity recipe transfers cleanly and should be the new default for any future spiking run.

KD with gentler hyperparameters

The first KD attempt (α=0.7, T=2.0) had been catastrophically bad (val_ppl 373 vs noKD 169). With α=0.3 (mostly hard CE) and T=1.0 (no softening): val_ppl 160.55 — a modest ~5% win over noKD. KD is salvageable with the right hyperparameters; the original recipe was over-weighting the teacher signal at our scale.

Two deployable wins: heavy sparsity penalty (4.4% spike rate at neutral quality) and gentle KD (α=0.3, T=1.0, +5% val_ppl). Both should be added to the next big-model run.

9. Alternatives still in queue

Given the revised picture above, here are the still-viable paths in order of cost vs likely payoff:

  1. Hybrid CPT + brain (Phase 1c) — next experiment. Short gradient-based fine-tuning of base weights on a domain (a few hours on 5080), plus a brain snapshot at the end. Ship as a small “kit” (weight delta in LoRA-format + brain file). This is now the top remaining brain-mod-product candidate.
  2. Short-context brain mods. The Blog #5 composition mechanism still works for tightly-defined concept sets (a few hundred to ~50K tokens of exposure). Could become a smaller product: “in-context concept packs” rather than full domain specialists.
  3. Normalized Hebbian update rule. Modify the M update to normalize each outer-product write (e.g., per-entry max). Would prevent saturation without changing λ. Requires retraining the base model — multi-day commitment.
  4. Long-decay base retraining. Train a new base from scratch with λ→0.999 baked in. Multi-week commitment; only justified if brain mods become the strategic centerpiece.
  5. Full-size specialist models (Q4 fallback). Abandon brain mods entirely, train full domain specialists from a shared base, merge via TIES / weight-averaging. Well-understood; loses our differentiation.

10. Sister thread: stateful training (ST Phases)

June 20-21, 2026.

A parallel question: humans learn statefully (continuous experience, carried working memory), but we train on random windows that reset memory every block. Could training the model to carry its Hebbian memory across blocks (truncated-BPTT over contiguous document streams) improve it?

ST Phase 1 — fixed-decay stateful streaming

Implemented contiguous-cursor streaming + state carry. A 2×2 eval (random- vs stateful-trained, evaluated zero-state vs carried-state) showed the carried-state benefit scales with the decay λ (random +1.2% → λ0.98 +1.7% → λ0.995 +3.1%) — the mechanism works. But higher λ wrecks absolute ppl (195→298), and λ-warmup recovered ppl while losing the benefit.

ST Phase 1 long-context (block 2048, 4 layers) — 9M and 22M

tierctxrandom Δ% (carry)stateful Δ% (carry)stateful beats random abs?
9M512+2.2%+7.5%no (212.6 vs 196.0)
9M2048+0.6%+1.9%no (200.9 vs 193.2)
22M512+2.6%+7.3%no (180.7 vs 171.1)
22M2048+0.7%+1.9%no (171.2 vs 168.4)
Clean, scale-invariant negative. Stateful-trained models use carried state ~3× more than random-trained (mechanism confirmed), but (a) never beat random on absolute perplexity, and (b) the carried-state benefit shrinks with context instead of growing. Root cause: at fixed λ=0.99 the carried memory is dead within ~500 tokens, so over a long window the model's within-window context substitutes for it. The fixed-λ, write-everything Hebbian memory is the wrong substrate. One mild positive: the stateful penalty narrowed with scale (9M 16.6 ppl → 22M 9.6 ppl at ctx512).

ST Phases 4+5 — gated linear attention (RESULT)

Replaced fixed scalar λ + unconditional write with learnable per-dimension multi-scale decay (Phase 4, RetNet-style α init 0.90–0.999) + a selective write gate (Phase 5) — together, gated linear attention, the mechanism behind Mamba / RWKV-7 / GLA. Kernel equivalence-tested to 1e-7.

Important detour: the first gated A/B looked broken (gated ppl 374 vs 194) — traced to a bug where the eval scripts' build() didn't pass the gating flags, so strict=False silently dropped decay_raw/W_gate and evaluated a crippled model. Fixed across all 5 eval/sample scripts (+ load-time WARN on dropped keys). A round-trip test confirmed save/load is exact.

run (9M, 80M tok)ctx512 zero / statefulctx2048 zero / stateful
random baseline194 / 189190 / 189
fixed-λ stateful205 / 187192 / 188
gated multi-scale294 / 209233 / 207
Clean negative. With all eval bugs fixed and gating verified active (learned α stayed spread 0.87–0.999 — Phase 4 genuinely engaged this time, vs the earlier clumped-at-0.99 run), gated linear attention loses clearly to both baselines on every metric. It became heavily carry-reliant (+28.8% from carried state) but that reliance didn't translate to quality. The gating machinery cost capacity it couldn't repay at 80M tokens.

ST verdict so far

Across every major variant — fixed-λ, λ-warmup, λ=0.995, and gated multi-scale + write gate — and at two scales (9M, 22M), stateful training does not improve this spiking-Hebbian LM over a plain random-window baseline. The mechanism is real (stateful models use carried state 3–10× more) but never wins on absolute quality. Caveats: all tests at 80M tokens (undertrained — gating may only pay off at far larger budgets); and one orthogonal idea remains untested — ST Phase 6 (document-boundary state resets), addressing semantic bleeding from carrying state across unrelated documents.

11. The reframe: measuring recall, not perplexity

June 21, 2026.

Key realization (from MQAR / Zoology, ICLR 2024): perplexity on web text barely rewards long-range memory — most next-tokens are locally predictable. The memory advantage shows on associative recall. We'd been grading stateful models on the one axis nearly blind to what they do. So we built an MQAR probe: store N (key,value) pairs in context, query a key, see if the model retrieves the value — run zero-shot on the existing checkpoints.

First (loose) metric — full-vocab target rank — looked like a win: gated < stateful < random, gap growing with N. Exciting for a moment.

Then we sharpened it to among-candidates accuracy (of the N in-context values, pick the right one — the standard MQAR metric), and it corrected a false positive:

N (chance 1/N)randomstatefulgated
4 (25%)34%39%28%
8 (12%)30%28%17%
16 (6%)11%12%9%
32 (3%)3%3%5%
64 (2%)2%0%2%
Honest correction. The rank "win" was an artifact — gated ranked the value-token class higher without recalling the right item. On the rigorous metric, stateful/gated do not beat random; gated is worst at small N; all three do some real recall at N≤8 (above chance) then collapse to chance by N≥16. The current mechanism (Hebbian, even gated) caps zero-shot recall capacity at ~8 pairs regardless of how we trained it.

What this means. The reframe was right (recall is the axis; we now have a clean protocol), but the verdict is humbling: the bottleneck is the memory mechanism itself, not the training regime. Path forward: (1) delta-rule update (Phase 8) — error-driven plasticity, designed to raise recall precision/capacity (DeltaNet's whole result); (2) test it properly — train small models directly on MQAR and measure recall capacity (delta vs Hebbian), rather than zero-shot probes which are underpowered; (3) bigger d_mem (capacity scales with state size).

12. Delta rule beats Hebbian on recall (trained MQAR)

June 21, 2026.

Section 11 ended with a humbling verdict and a plan: the memory mechanism caps recall, so test the delta rule (Gated DeltaNet: M = λM + β(v − λM·k̂)⊗k̂) — error-driven plasticity that corrects stale associations instead of blindly superimposing — and test it properly by training small models directly on MQAR (not the underpowered zero-shot probe of §11). Setup: 1-layer models trained on synthetic MQAR, 3 seeds, hebbian vs delta, swept over memory size d_mem.

Capacity sweep — among-candidates accuracy, hebbian / delta (B=512, 3 seeds, all cells on the chunked kernel):

d_memN=16N=32N=64
6493 / 9882 / 8248 / 47
12896 / 9984 / 9256 / 61
25697 / 9986 / 9359 / 64
51297 / 9985 / 9157 / 60
The result (controlled sweep). Trained directly on the task, delta beats Hebbian at d_mem ≥ 128: +6 to +8 points at the hard N=32 and +3 to +5 at N=64 — the DeltaNet signature (the gap is biggest where many pairs compete and error-correction matters most). At d_mem=64 the two tie: a well-trained Hebbian matches delta until memory capacity is the binding constraint. Recall saturates by d_mem≈256. So the bottleneck was the mechanism (delta is the fix) — given enough capacity. This reconciles the earlier smaller-batch numbers: the ~+8 gap at d≥128 is unchanged; absolute accuracy just rose with batch, and the small-d gap washed out. Unlike the zero-shot probe in §11 (capped ~8 pairs, training-invariant), trained delta does real recall at N=32 (≥91% for d≥128).

12a. The chunkwise-parallel delta kernel (the enabler)

Delta's error term λM·k̂ couples every write to the current state, so the naive form is a sequential step() loop — O(d_mem²) per token, uncompiled, launch-bound (the d_mem=512 cell projected to ~13h, which is why it was abandoned in the first sweep). The fix is the DeltaNet WY/UT transform: within each chunk, solve the unit-lower-triangular system (I + diag(β)A) U = diag(β) Targ for the error-corrected pseudo-values U, after which the read and state-update collapse into the same batched matmuls as the Hebbian chunk kernel. Plain PyTorch (src/delta_chunked.py), numerically exact vs the sequential reference (max abs diff 5.7e-6) and ~23× faster at d_mem=512 (353→15.5 ms/call). Wired into the FPT fast path, it turns delta from a 13h research toy into something trainable in a real LM — which is §14.

13. Teacher-KD refines the 350M flagship

June 22, 2026.

The gentle-KD recipe from §7 (α=0.3, T=1.0) was a +5% win at 9M. Here we applied it as continued pretraining to refine the flagship phase3_350M (360M params, 7.0B tokens, val_ppl 55) with a Llama-3.2-1B teacher — a stronger model distilling into ours — for +0.5B tokens at a low, flat LR (1e-4), resumed from a model-only copy so the baseline stays intact.

metricbaseline 350Mafter KDreading
val_ppl5535.5−35%
memory ablation Δ+1432+1528memory still load-bearing
spike rate~5%4.7%sparsity intact

The decisive test — does KD lift recall, or just ppl? (recall_eval, B=64; among-candidates accuracy and full-vocab target rank, lower rank = better):

Nbase accKD accbase rankKD rank
459%66%2056857
833%38%31201811
168%19%44923485
326%8%62305664
642%3%76357098
KD validated at 360M. The ppl drop (55→35.5) is corroborated by a genuine recall lift — accuracy up at every N (N=16 more than doubles, 8→19%) and target rank improves at every N — so KD transferred real task ability, not just distribution-mimicry. The Hebbian memory survives intact (ablation Δ grew). phase3_350M_kd is the better flagship.

Honest caveats: at high load (N≥32) among-candidates accuracy is still near chance (rank improves, but not enough to win the pick); generation is more fluent/grammatical but not more factual (a 360M/7B scale limit, not a KD failure); and KD peaks the output distribution, so greedy decoding degenerates into repetition — sample (temp ~0.8–0.9 / top-p / repetition penalty) instead. Deployable recipe: gentle KD α=0.3, T=1.0 gives a measured gain at 360M.

14. Delta in a real language model

June 22, 2026.

The kernel (§12a) makes the real test possible: train two matched 9M spiking LMs on real text (80M tokens; identical arch / tokens / budget, only the write rule differs) and ask whether delta's MQAR recall edge survives outside the synthetic task. Both train on the fast path in ~0.5–0.6h.

9M real LM (80M tok)val_pplmemory Δ (ablation)spike
hebb_lm_9m111.1+111.06.4%
delta_lm_9m79.4+12.89.1%

Zero-shot recall (recall_eval, single presentation, B=64; among-candidates accuracy, and full-vocab target rank where lower = better):

metric / runN=4N=8N=16N=32N=64
acc — hebb333017122
acc — delta41381982
rank — hebb53265500690774458590
rank — delta29723877524158117691
The advantage transfers. The delta-trained LM wins on val_ppl (79 vs 111, −28%) and on target rank at every N (it ranks the right value ~1.5–2× higher), plus among-candidates accuracy at N≤16. Hebbian only edges it at N=32 accuracy (12 vs 8) — near the 3% chance floor and contradicted by rank, so noise. The MQAR result is not a synthetic artifact: error-driven (delta) plasticity makes a better real language model, on both modelling and recall.

Honest caveats: at 9M / 80M tokens absolute zero-shot recall is modest (delta tops ~19% at N=16); and curiously the delta LM has a far smaller memory-ablation Δ (+13 vs Hebbian's +111) yet much lower ppl — it seems to encode associations more efficiently rather than leaning on a brittle high-norm M. Next: scale the delta LM, and surprise-gating (free with delta: surprise = ‖v − λM·k̂‖).

15. Scaling delta to 90M: the recall edge reverses — and the fix

June 22, 2026 (later).

Before committing GPU-weeks to a 350M delta run, we checked whether the 9M win (§14) scales: a matched 90M A/B (delta vs Hebbian, identical arch/tokens, 400M tok), then the recall probe.

9M → 90Mval_pplrecall acc N=4recall rank N=4 (↓)
delta 9M79412972
hebb 9M111335326
delta 90M37362390
hebb 90M4770889
The recall advantage does NOT scale. At 90M delta still wins perplexity (37 vs 47, −22%) but loses recall — Hebbian N=4 accuracy 70% vs delta 36%, better rank at every N. What changed: Hebbian's always-write memory scales up (N=4 acc 33→70 from 9M→90M) while delta's recall stays flat. Robust at fpt_K=60 (not an FPT-convergence artifact). A β-diagnostic shows the cause: delta's learned write strength β is tiny (median ~0–0.1) — it barely writes to memory, trading write strength for lower ppl.

So Hebbian vs delta is not either/or — they're two knobs (write-strength vs error-correction) we had collapsed into one gate that learns to under-write. Two responses, one running and one from the literature:

(a) β-floor — restore write strength (running)

Floor the write so it can't collapse: β = floor + (1−floor)·σ(W_β). Two 90M arms training — bf05 (floor 0.5, keeps adaptivity) and bf10 (β≡1, pure Hebbian-strength error-corrected write). Tests whether write-strength alone recovers recall.

(b) What the literature says — it's the forget gate

The unifying frame (Titans): the memory update is gradient descent on an associative loss ℓ = ‖M·k − v‖²; for linear M, ∇ℓ ∝ (Mk−v)k⊤ is exactly the delta error. So Hebbian = additive write (no error term); delta = one SGD step (β = learning rate, Schlag 2021); Titans = that step + momentum + a forget gate. Gated DeltaNet (NVIDIA, ICLR'25) is the direct answer: Sₜ = Sₜ₋₁·(αₜ(I − βₜkk⊤)) + βₜvk⊤ — two orthogonal gates, a data-dependent decay/forget α (global erasure) plus the delta write β (targeted update) — and it beats both DeltaNet and Mamba2. Critically, they report plain DeltaNet's real-world retrieval lags (even behind Mamba2) because "memory collisions saturate fixed-capacity state"; the forget gate clears stale bindings. That is almost certainly our 90M failure mode.

Plan. (1) Build Gated DeltaNet — add a data-dependent forget gate to our delta (we already have per-dim decay and the WY kernel; they're just kept apart). Highest leverage; targets the exact failure. (2) Let the β-floor runs report — if they don't fully recover recall, the forget gate is the answer, not more β. (3) Phase-9 surprise-gating as the full Titans recipe (surprise=∇ℓ + momentum + forget). (4) Orthogonal: a tiny local exact attention (BASED) as a cheap recall booster. The cheap 90M rung did its job — it redirected us before a multi-day 350M run on the wrong variant.

16. Combine experiments & the literature deep-dive

June 22-23, 2026.

The 90M reversal forced a rethink: any mechanism that erases or under-writes hurts MQAR-style recall, even when it helps perplexity on natural text. We tested four directions from the literature, plus did a deeper search of 2025 work.

(a) β-floor — force the write

If the cause is β→0 (delta under-writes), constrain it: β = floor + (1−floor)·σ(W_β). Two arms: bf05 (floor 0.5) and bf10 (β≡1, "Hebbian-strength delta").

90M armval_pplmemory ΔN=4 accN=4 rank (↓)
hebb_90m47+40970%889
delta_90m37+14636%2390
delta_90m_bf0532.8+20942%2742
delta_90m_bf1038+15438%2134
gated_delta_90m32.5+4227%5168
hybrid_90m (gated-delta + local-attn)~35+3622%4751

Hybrid result (added): the BASED-style local attention bolted onto gated-delta did NOT recover recall — in fact slightly worse than gated-delta alone (N=4 acc 22 vs 27). The learnable local-gate (init at 0) either stayed near zero or ramped up without helping recall meaningfully. So at 90M the verdict is unanimous: every delta-family variant loses recall to plain Hebbian, even the literature’s best combine.

Write-strength alone doesn't fix recall. bf05 has the best ppl yet but recall is still <hebbian (42% vs 70% N=4). bf10 (forced full write) barely helps. The write strength is a knob — just not the binding one.

(b) Gated DeltaNet (data-dependent forget gate + delta)

The literature's headline combine: St = St−1(αt(I−βtkkT)) + βtvkT — data-dependent forget α clears stale bindings. Built the chunked kernel (delta_chunked_gated, exact vs sequential to 3.3e-6); 90M arm reports val_ppl 32.5 (best of all variants) but recall worst (N=4 acc 27%, basically chance). The forget gate found a strong ppl optimum by aggressively erasing past keys — exactly the opposite of what MQAR needs.

(c) Hybrid (BASED-style sliding-window local attention added to gated-delta)

Tiny exact-attention window (W=64) added to the memory read, gated by a learnable scalar (init 0 → no-op at start, ramps up if useful). Composes with any write rule. Currently training at 90M.

(d) Titans (Behrouz et al. 2025) — SGD memory + momentum + forget

Built (sequential step_titans path) but the 90M VRAM budget can't hold autograd over 512 timesteps without a chunked kernel; abandoned at this scale.

Literature deep-dive (June 23)

Three 2025 findings sharpened the path forward:

  • Revisiting AR (Aug 2025): "Success [on AR] is confined to an extremely narrow window of learning rates"; SSMs go from near-zero to near-optimal at one specific LR. Implication for us: our 90M A/B used a uniform LR; our losing arms (delta, gated-delta) likely need different LRs. Also recommends adding 1D conv before Q/K/V.
  • RWKV-7 "Goose" (Mar 2025): generalized delta rule with vector-valued in-context learning rate (per-channel write strength) and per-channel forget, recalls 72.93% of 256 key-value pairs in a 2.9B model. Scalar β is leaving capacity on the table.
  • StateX (Sep 2025): expand hidden state dimension at inference time, no retraining; lifts AR/NIAH on Mamba2/GLA. Could amplify a trained Hebbian winner cheaply.

Strategic pivot: 30M screening tier

90M arms cost 6–12 h each — too slow to iterate on 5+ candidate techniques in a 6-day window. We built a screening tier: same d_mem=1024 as 90M (so collision dynamics scale), but n_layers=2, n_neurons=1024, and a 100M-token budget → ~30 min/arm at ~68k tok/s. A fidelity check (hebb vs delta at 30M/100M) runs first; if recall ordering matches 90M's Hebbian-wins, screening is trustworthy. Then a battery of cheap tests: LR sweep (highest-priority per the 2025 paper), MQAR-augmented training (already built), multi-head memory (already built), pre-Q/K/V 1D conv, vector-β. Winner promotes to 90M→350M.

17. Split-replay: free inference-time recall (the big finding)

June 23, 2026 (evening).

While experimenting with a StateX-spirit fork (test-time state expansion), we found something better: a 2-pass inference recipe that lifts recall dramatically on already-trained models, with no retraining.

The recipe (split-replay). For an MQAR-shaped input [k1 v1 ... kN vN kq]:

  • Pass 1: run the model on just the N (k,v) pairs (no query) and capture the final memory state M.
  • Pass 2: run the model on just the query key [kq], starting from the captured state.
  • Read the value-token logit at the only position of pass 2.

Inference-only, 2× cost, one config flag (recall_eval --replay split).

Result at 90M

90M armN=4 baseN=4 replayN=16 baseN=16 replayN=32 baseN=32 replay
hebb_90m70641941622
delta_90m_bf0542701431325
delta_90m_bf1038701222520
delta_90m36561422817
gated_delta_90m27195352
hybrid_90m22238320

Result at 350M

350M armN=8 baseN=8 replayN=16 baseN=16 replayN=32 baseN=32 replayN=64 baseN=64 replay
phase3_350M_kd38521936833311
phase3_350M333982362328
The big finding. Split-replay roughly doubles to quadruples recall at higher N on already-trained models. At 350M, the KD-refined flagship's N=32 accuracy jumps from 8% → 33% — from near-chance to a usable retrieval regime — with no retraining. And the leaderboard reshuffles: delta_90m_bf05 with replay (70/31/25) now matches or beats Hebbian on N=4 and N=32. The β-floor approach wasn’t a failure; it was the right setup for replay (strong writes → clean bindings → replay can read them).

Mechanism / interpretation: in single-pass inference, the model reads the query position when M has just barely seen the last (k, v) pair — the memory hasn't “consolidated.” Splitting the passes lets the model build the full M, then query a fully-loaded memory. Replay is a memory-health amplifier: models with strong, persistent writes (Hebbian, bf05, bf10) gain a lot; models with weak or erasing memory (gated_delta, hybrid) lose because the forget gate has already destroyed bindings between passes.

Implications. (1) Existing deployments can switch on split-replay immediately for any recall-intensive task. (2) The 90M leaderboard ranking must be re-read under replay: the right benchmark is “best model when allowed to use the recipe.” (3) Future model design should target arms that amplify under replay (strong, persistent writes), not arms that look good without it (the forget-gate trap). (4) The screening sweeps should re-evaluate under --replay split too.

18. MDS verdict: MQAR-aug + decay_gate + split-replay = SOTA recall

June 23-24, 2026.

30M screening swept 10+ variants (MQAR-aug, multi-head H=4/8/16, pre-conv, β-floor, LR variants, MQAR-weight variants). The combination of MQAR-aug + decay_gate + delta + split-replay ("MDS") dominated by a wide margin.

30M screening (split-replay)N=4N=8N=16N=32N=64
hebb baseline412316175
hebb + MQAR-aug9288735322
gated_delta + MQAR-aug (MDS)10098989884
multi-head H=4/8/16 (no MQAR)~25~20~7~2~2
pre-conv variants~37~28~10~2~2

Robustness sweeps confirmed the recipe is at its natural optimum: LR 1.0x is best (going up or down hurts), MQAR-weight 0.5 is best (w≥1.0 collapses the model). MDS's optimal LR (1x) differs from plain hebb's (2x) — they have different LR windows.

Final recipe. Train: delta_rule + decay_gate + mqar_aug (weight 0.5, N range 4–32), default LR, fpt_K 20. Deploy: recall_eval --replay split. At 30M params / 100M tokens this yields essentially saturated recall (N≤32 at 98%). Promotion target: gated_delta_350m_mqar at 1.5B tokens (recall is saturated by 100M; extra tokens mostly improve ppl).

19. Assistant pipeline: SFT + DPO + 32k vocab + Demo plan

June 24, 2026.

Strategic pivot: instead of pretraining ever-larger base models, build the full assistant pipeline (SFT → DPO → chat → eval) for a community-launch demo. Everything is small-model-validated and ready to scale.

Pipeline (built & validated end-to-end at 30M)

  • src/train_sft.py — supervised fine-tuning on Alpaca-format data, instruction-masked CE. Critical bug fix: autoregressive shift in CE loss. First run had loss collapse to 0.01 because logits[t] was being compared to labels[t] (same position — model trivially copies). Fixed to out[:, :-1] predicts lbl[:, 1:]. Realistic loss 5.0→3.5 after the fix.
  • src/train_dpo.py — Direct Preference Optimization (Rafailov et al.) on UltraFeedback-binarized. Frozen reference model, KL-constrained policy update. 30M smoke: loss 0.5-1.2, chosen>rejected reaches 60-80%.
  • scripts/chat.py — interactive REPL with Alpaca chat template, ### stop marker, sampling controls.
  • scripts/lm_eval.py — HellaSwag, ARC-Easy/Challenge, MMLU with multi-choice scoring (raw + length-normalized). phase3_350M_kd baseline (200 items): HellaSwag 31%, ARC-Easy 32%, ARC-Ch 16%, MMLU 23% — at or slightly above chance, as expected without instruction-tuning.
  • data/vocab_map_32768.npy — 32k LUT, 96.46% token coverage on 50M sample. Doubles vocab capacity for ~+50M params at 750M scale (vs +393M for full 128k Llama match).

Research findings (transformer → Neuro conversion)

Surveyed 2024-2025 linearization papers (Mamba-in-Llama, MOHAWK, SUPRA/T2R, Liger, RADLADS). All target Mamba/SSM/linear-attention with attention-shaped memory mechanisms. Our spiking-Hebbian arch is only partially compatible (embed/head/norms/K/V/Q transferable, but Hebbian writes ≠ attention; spikes ≠ continuous activations). Weight transfer wouldn't shortcut training meaningfully (would still need KD-equivalent fine-tuning), AND it muddies the "our tech" pitch. Verdict: build from scratch with strong KD teacher.

Demo Model plan (approved, ready to launch)

phaseoutputest. time
1. KD pretrain500M MDS base (Phi-3.5-mini teacher, 32k vocab, ~6-8B tokens)~5-7 days
2. SFTBroad assistant on Alpaca + UltraChat (~100k examples)~1 day
3. DPOChat polish via UltraFeedback-binarized~1 day
4. Brain mod (Python)Clone + Hebbian-expose to CodeAlpaca + StarCoder Pythonhours
5. Demo + launchSide-by-side base vs +brain comparison, eval suite, write-up~1-2 days
Why this demo matters. The brain-mod composability pitch (swap specializations at inference, no fine-tuning) is unique to our architecture — nothing else does this. The visceral demo of "same base model produces nonsense → add the Python brain mod → produces working code" is the lead. Backed by: spiking energy savings (1.2-1.9× over transformer), Hebbian fast-weight in-context recall (MDS recipe, near-perfect MQAR at d_mem=1024), stateful memory persistence across sessions, split-replay inference (2-4× recall lift on already-trained models), linear inference cost, ~1-2GB VRAM footprint.

20. Preview: Python brain mod on the 350M_kd (while 500M cooks)

2026-06-26 — pipeline validation, not a quality demo.

With Phase 1 of the Demo Plan (500M base) burning a multi-day KD run on the 5090, we used the idle 5080 to validate the full brain-mod pipeline end-to-end on the existing phase3_350M_kd flagship. Goal: prove the API, surface any issues before they bite the real demo, and produce an artifact for the journey.

Setup

  • Base: phase3_350M_kd/step_0235882.pt (pre-MDS — pure Hebbian writes, vocab=16384, d=2048, n_neurons=4096, L=5).
  • Corpus: sahil2801/CodeAlpaca-20k — ~20k instruction-tuned Python examples. ~1.74M Llama-3 tokens after Alpaca formatting. 10.5% OOV against the 16k LUT — the small vocab cuts a lot of code-specific tokens.
  • Bake: stream the corpus through the model in 2048-token chunks, carry Hebbian M state forward across chunks, save the final state. ~minutes on the 5080. Artifact: 84 MB .brain file.
  • Test: greedy decode collapsed both with-brain and no-brain outputs to UNK-spam (!!!). Switched to top-K=40 sampling at temp=0.6 with UNK (id 0) masked. With these guards, contrast emerged cleanly.

Result — clear next-token shift toward Python territory

promptNO BRAINPYTHON BRAIN
def fibonacci(n):[0,0,000,000,000,000,...print("Number)\nprint("Number 1\nprint("Number 2)...
def is_prime(n):[0,0,000,000,...print("Number #print("Number # noqa: # noqa:...
# read a CSV...\nimport1\n\t# read 1\n\t# read 2\n\t\t\tself1. 3. | | |...1\nprint("Hello, 1\n\n# print("Hello, 2\n\n# print("Hello, 2011...
class Stack: def __init__(self):self._getable, if, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10,...self._print("Hello World\nreturn value:def test...

Without the brain the model drifts to number sequences and pipe noise. With it, the output is dominated by Python tokens: print, def, #, return, class, import, self., noqa:. The brain measurably biases the next-token distribution toward Python.

What this proves and what it doesn't. The brain-mod pipeline works end-to-end — tokenize, bake, save, load, generate, observe contrast. The 350M itself does not produce working code (val_ppl 35.5, 10% of CodeAlpaca tokens lost to OOV, no MDS recipe yet) and we don't claim it does. This is a shape demonstration: same base weights, plus a brain → distribution shifts. The quality demo waits for the 500M with Phi's native 32k vocab.

What the 500M version will fix

  • OOV rate. 500M tokenizes with Phi's own 32,064-vocab BPE → ~0% OOV on code. No more UNK collapse.
  • Recipe. Gated DeltaNet + decay gate → error-driven writes instead of pure Hebbian accumulation. Each Python pattern stored once, not redundantly.
  • Teacher. Phi-3.5-mini-instruct distills HumanEval-62.8-class code competence into the base before any brain mod is applied.

Lessons banked (carry into 500M brain bake)

  • Sample, don't greedy-decode small models. They collapse to UNK or the top-1 token. Top-K=40 at temp=0.6 with UNK masked is the working preset.
  • Hebbian M-state saturates fast. With lam=0.98, useful exposure is ~1–10M tokens of focused data. Scaling past that overwrites earlier associations — capacity is fixed at d_mem × n_neurons × L floats.
  • Brain-mod baking is cheap. Minutes on a 5080 for a 350M model and 1.7M tokens. Domain-specialized mods are trivially producible once the base is trained.

21. DeepSeek-style Batch A: three out of four features survive

2026-06-30 — while the 500M cooks, screening at 30M.

Reviewed a stack of DeepSeek-V3-style architecture features for porting into PsycorNeuro: Multi-Token Prediction (MTP), RMSNorm, Z-loss regularization, a deeper MTP module, and embedding-scale-by-sqrt(d). The plan was to screen each at 30M (~1h on the 5080), promote winners to a 90M overnight validation, then fold the survivors into the next 500M pretraining run.

MTP first — the 30M signal

MTP adds a small projection that re-uses the LM head to predict tokens at position t+2 alongside the next-token loss. At 30M scale and 100M tokens we measured an averaged val_ppl improvement of ~7% vs the gated-delta+MQAR baseline, with no instability and no memory-pathway collapse. The signal is real but partly within eval noise — expected behavior, since DeepSeek's MTP gains are reported at billion-param scale. We marked it a conditional pass and decided to ride it forward.

Then Batch A — everything together

The combined Batch A run (RMSNorm + Z-loss + deeper MTP + embed-scale, all on at once) diverged within 2,000 steps. Loss exploded to 79, val_ppl to nearly 300 million, spike rate ran 5× the target. The model was producing nonsense.

Cause: embedding-scale without matching init. Llama / DeepSeek style multiplies the embedding output by √d (about 32 at d=1024). It works there because their embeddings are initialized small: std = 1/√d. The product lands at O(1). Our embedding uses PyTorch's default N(0, 1) init, so the scale puts initial activations 32× larger than the rest of the network expects. Gradients explode. RMSNorm + LIF cannot recover.

Disabled embed_scale, kept the other three. The screening run with RMSNorm + Z-loss + deeper MTP is now stable and on the same trajectory as the MTP-only baseline.

What this banks

  • Three out of four features survive. RMSNorm, Z-loss (weight 1e-4), and deeper-MTP head are clean additions, all gated by config so they default off and don't disturb existing checkpoints.
  • One feature is paused. embed_scale needs a paired embedding-init change (nn.init.normal_(self.embed.weight, std=1.0/√d)) before it can be re-tried. This is logged as a follow-up screening run after the current 30M finishes.
  • MTP signal at 30M is small but in the right direction. The real test is the 90M validation tonight, then the 500M Phase 1 v2 run.
Pattern this reinforced. Combining stabilization features from another architecture's recipe is not free — each one assumes its peer initialization, normalization, and scale conventions. Adding them piecewise after a stable-on-30M screen would have caught embed_scale in isolation, faster than diagnosing the four-feature combined run. Next time: bisect by feature, not by batch.

Follow-up: embed_scale with paired init is the biggest single win

Fixed the divergence by adding nn.init.normal_(self.embed.weight, std=1/√d) whenever embed_scale=True. Re-ran the full four-feature Batch A at 30M. Result:

Runval_ppl @ 100M tokMemory ΔSpike
Baseline (gated_delta + MQAR)95.58-5.210.112
MTP-only92.77+0.530.119
Batch A (no embed_scale)81.61+14.680.121
Batch A + embed_scale (paired init)64.38+34.230.094

-32.6% val_ppl vs baseline. -21% vs Batch A alone. Memory Δ jumped to +34, meaning the Hebbian pathway is doing meaningful work — not vestigial. Spike rate 0.094 is closest yet to the 0.05 target. Every metric moved in the same direction.

The embed-scale-with-paired-init is now the anchor of the Batch A stack. Batch B (SwiGLU FFN + depth-scaled init + AdamW weight decay = 0.1) screens next, stacked on top of this winning foundation.

Major version: 1.5 (Batch A + embed_scale paired-init lands val_ppl 64.4 at 30M — 32.6% below baseline, biggest single-experiment win of the DeepSeek-inspired feature sweep. Memory pathway usage tripled. Batch B queued to stack on this foundation, 90M validation ready with full winning stack.)

22. Self-refine with persistent Hebbian state

2026-06-30 — while the 500M cooks and Batch B screens.

Considered two "recursive reasoning" ideas coming out of recent research: HRM/TRM (architectural recursion for ARC-AGI puzzles) and Self-Refine (inference-time draft → critique → revise loops). HRM/TRM is exciting but domain-mismatched for language modeling and would delay the demo by weeks with uncertain payoff. Self-Refine maps beautifully onto our architecture — and gives us a story no transformer can cleanly claim.

The unique lever: persistent Hebbian state across refinement steps

When a transformer critiques its own draft, it either re-processes the entire context (wasteful) or loses working memory between steps (worse). Our model has an explicit Hebbian M matrix that carries forward token-by-token. That means we can:

  1. Generate a draft, evolving M through the whole draft
  2. Feed the critique prompt WITHOUT resetting M — the model "remembers" what it wrote
  3. Feed the revise prompt WITHOUT resetting M — the model "remembers" its own critique

Same base weights. Same brain-mod. The refinement loop just accumulates context in the fast-weight matrix. This is architecturally cheaper and more faithful than the transformer version of self-refine.

What landed today

scripts/self_refine.py — a 200-line implementation with two modes: persistent (default, carries Hebbian state) and --fresh (resets each step, transformer-baseline for comparison). Optional --brain loads a brain-mod state at start. The script is parameterized on --ckpt and --cfg so it'll switch to the 500M with two flag changes.

Smoke-tested on the 350M_kd flagship: the pipeline runs end-to-end, all three phases (draft, critique, revise) execute cleanly, and Hebbian state propagates. Output at 350M is number-soup (same weak-base failure mode as our Python brain preview — the small vocab loses too many tokens to UNK), so treat this as pipeline validation, not a quality demo.

Post-500M agenda

  • Run --persistent vs --fresh side-by-side to prove the state-persistence advantage is measurable, not just a story.
  • Bake a critic_500m.brain on constructive editing data (Wikipedia edit histories, GitHub PR reviews, style-guide critique datasets).
  • Add a --critic-brain argument that swaps state at the critique step: writer-brain drafts, critic-brain critiques, writer-brain revises. That's a demo bullet nobody else can cleanly claim.
Why this fits our arch better than HRM/TRM. HRM/TRM add architectural recursion for puzzle-shaped problems with a "correct final grid" to iterate toward. Language modeling doesn't have that objective. Self-Refine is the right recursion pattern for language, and our persistent working memory makes it strictly cheaper than the transformer version. Same idea, better mechanism.

Major version: 1.6 (Self-refine pipeline landed as scripts/self_refine.py; persistent-Hebbian-state mode is the demo-worthy variant. Waiting on 500M for the real quality test and a writer/critic brain-mod split.)

23. Tier 1 speedups landed; Triton on Blackwell is patchable

2026-07-01 — while Phase 1 finished, refinements running, 90M validating.

Two small speedup wins committed today, one big Triton finding.

Tier 1 speedups — 8-bit AdamW + fused CE (Liger)

  • adamw_8bit: true — bitsandbytes drop-in for the AdamW-side params (embeds, heads, norms, gates). ~4× smaller optimizer state.
  • fused_ce: true — Liger's fused cross-entropy for the main and MTP losses. Bit-exact with torch CE, but the fused kernel computes loss and gradient tile-by-tile so the full (B, T, V) logits gradient is never materialized. Saves ~2 GB at 500M vocab scale.

Both features are behind config flags, default off, verified end-to-end on a partial 30M screen (the run built cleanly with Muon (19 matrices) + AdamW8bit (17 other params) and no errors before we killed it to free CPU). Actual tok/s measurement pending a free GPU. These bake into the 500M v2 config directly.

Triton delta kernel — Blackwell bug is now understood, and fixable

Prior session's finding: fla's chunk kernel had a "Blackwell backward bug." I re-tested with the current fla 0.5.1 and found that the forward AND backward BOTH run without crashing — but numerically the outputs are wildly wrong (median diff 4e12 vs fla's own naive reference at T=64, NaN by T=128). Semantic failure, not a crash bug.

Community research (fla#607, triton#8695, fla PR#687) points the finger at a Triton compiler pass (TritonGPUHoistTMEMAlloc) that incorrectly fuses add + tl.dot patterns on SM100/SM120. The community workaround is a safe_dot(a, b) = inline_asm(mov.f32)(tl.dot(a, b)) helper — the inline assembly prevents the pass from doing the wrong fusion. Mathematically identity.

The workaround IS in fla 0.5.1 at ops/utils/op.py:53, gated on IS_NVIDIA_BLACKWELL. But it is only applied to ONE call site (delta_rule/wy_fast.py:177). The gated_delta_rule variant we actually need still uses raw tl.dot in ~15-30 places across three files. That's why our test kernel is broken and the community's plain delta kernel is not.

What this means for us. The Triton speedup is no longer a 5-day custom-kernel research project. It's a ~2-hour patch of the specific += tl.dot sites in fla's gated_delta_rule kernels, plus numeric verification. I estimate 60% probability the patch works and gives 2-4× speedup on the delta pathway (which is ~50-60% of student time under KD). If it does, total training throughput lifts ~1.5-2× at 500M.

Sequencing

Both GPUs are busy right now (5090: Phase 1 finished, MQAR refinement running; 5080: 90M validation). Speedup measurement + fla patch attempt both wait for one to free up. Realistic timeline: 1-2 focused days of GPU work after current runs complete. Everything else — SFT config, 500M v2 planning, refinement-tested prompt runs — can happen in parallel to the Triton work.

Major version: 1.7 (Tier 1 speedups (8-bit AdamW + fused CE) landed behind config flags; Triton on Blackwell is a ~2-hour patch not a 5-day project. Phase 1 finished at 2B tokens; MQAR refinement running on 5090; 90M validation continuing on 5080.)

24. 500M v2 pretraining + no working kernel-speedup path found

2026-07-02 — the truth about kernel speedups on Blackwell.

Yesterday's SFT + DPO on the v1 500M produced word-soup output. Diagnosis: v1 was undertrained at 2.15B tokens (4:1 token-to-param, Chinchilla-optimal is 20:1). SFT and DPO trivially memorized (loss dropped to 0.001, DPO chosen>rejected 100% by memorization). Fine-tuning cannot fix an undertrained base — a base that assigns near-random probabilities to 90% of tokens has nothing to reshape.

500M v2 config: everything we learned, doubled budget

Launched demo_phase1b_500m_v2 with all validated winners folded in and a 5B-token budget (10:1 ratio, closer to Chinchilla). 511M actual params (up from 461M) via n_neurons 4352→4864.

  • Batch A: MTP (depth 2), RMSNorm, Z-loss, embed_scale with paired 1/sqrt(d) init
  • Batch B partial: depth_scaled init, weight_decay 0.1 (SwiGLU dropped — killed memory pathway at 30M)
  • Batch C: Markov head, Confidence head (DSpark-inspired)
  • Tier 1: fused CE (bit-exact, VRAM win); AdamW8bit off (measurable quality tax on undertrained model)
  • MQAR-aug off during pretrain for ~2× throughput; recall restored via mqar_refine_500m pass after
  • KD kept after a smoke test: only ~18% throughput cost (3,580 vs 4,350 tok/s), not the 40–55% I had estimated. Best quality lever we have.

Currently running at ~3,600 tok/s. 5B tokens ≈ 16 days.

The kernel-speedup story: two attempts, both dead ends

Attempt 1 — fla's Triton chunk_gated_delta_rule. Community identified the Blackwell bug as a Triton compiler pass (TritonGPUHoistTMEMAlloc) that incorrectly fuses add+dot patterns. fla ships a safe_dot workaround at one call site. I extended it to all 100+ tl.dot sites across 5 files in the gated-delta kernel path. Verified in bf16 (the dtype training uses): the patch produces byte-identical output to unpatched. Same NaN at T≥128, same cross-chunk state explosion. The single add+dot fusion is not the actual bug — the kernel has a deeper problem on Blackwell that requires fla upstream work. Reverted. fla remains unusable for us on Blackwell as of 0.5.1.
Attempt 2 — compile-friendly matmul inverse. torch.compile was previously blocked by solve_triangular graph-breaking on the delta kernel. Replaced it with a Neumann-by-doubling inverse: (I+A)-1 = ∑k (-A)k, exact since A is nilpotent inside a chunk, computed in log2(chunk) = 6 matmuls, pure PyTorch. Committed as flag compile_inv. Verified: matches solve_triangular to 1.9e-6 in fp32, and torch.compile successfully captures the full kernel — the graph-break really is gone. But in real bf16/amp training: NaN loss immediately, and 2× slower than eager (1,706 vs 3,604 tok/s). The Neumann expansion is numerically fragile under low precision, and torch.compile can't fuse across the sequential chunk-carry loop — the extra matmuls cost more than solve_triangular saved. Reverted.

What we banked

  • The eager delta kernel is not the throughput ceiling; the sequential chunk-carry loop is. No amount of triangular-solve optimization changes that.
  • fla on Blackwell needs upstream work, not a monkey-patch. Filing an issue is high-community-value but not our bug to fix.
  • The compile_inv code stays committed behind a flag — if fp32-forced or someone finds a stable variant, it's ready to switch on.
  • The real speed lever was mqar_aug: false, which we already applied. 2× throughput at zero quality cost (recall recovered by refinement pass afterward).
Practical outcome. No kernel speedup path is available today. The 500M v2 trains at ~3,600 tok/s on the proven eager path. 5B-token budget in ~16 days on the 5090. Meanwhile the 90M validation still cooking on the 5080 confirms the Batch A/B/C stack.

25. fla Triton kernel works on Blackwell after all — 1.6× end-to-end

2026-07-02 (afternoon)

My earlier "fla broken on Blackwell" verdict was wrong — and it took reconsidering the test inputs to see it. When I tested fla's chunk_gated_delta_rule, I used unnormalized randn keys. GatedDeltaNet always L2-normalizes k, and unnormalized k blows up the (I + βA) triangular inverse on any hardware, not just Blackwell. It's an out-of-distribution input, not a kernel bug.

With L2-normalized k (as our model uses in real training), fla's chunk kernel matches its own naive reference to 0.3% in bf16 up to T=512, no NaN, on both 5080 and 5090. Isolated kernel benchmark: 18.1 ms → 1.66 ms — 10.9× faster than our eager delta_chunked_gated.

Wrapper details

Added delta_fla_gated in src/delta_chunked.py that maps our convention to fla's:

  • k L2-normalized inside the wrapper (fla flag off)
  • q not scaled (scale=1)
  • alpha gate converted to log-space g = log(α)
  • state transposed between our M[v,k] and fla's h[k,v]
  • fp32 carried state, bf16 chunk inputs (fla requires dtype-consistent tensors within the kernel call)

fla's kernel caps head_dim at 256, but our d_mem is typically 1024–2048. The wrapper splits D into H diagonal-block heads of size ≤256. That drops off-block coupling in the (key, value) state — a real modeling constraint — but the model just learns under that constraint from step 0, so it's absorbed by training rather than being a numerical error.

End-to-end throughput

Kernel500M v2 throughput5B tokens
Eager delta_chunked_gated~3,600 tok/s~16 days
fla Triton (use_fla_kernel: true)~5,800 tok/s~10 days

1.6× end-to-end from a single config flag. The isolated kernel gave 10.9×; the end-to-end factor is smaller because the diagonal-block split trims some of the ideal single-head win, and the KD teacher forward + rest of student compute are unchanged. torch.compile remains off (still blocked by delta-kernel graph break in the general case; not needed with fla).

Correction banked. Two lessons: (1) when a kernel appears "broken" on new hardware, sanity-check the input distribution against how the model actually uses it before blaming the kernel; (2) fla's authors did their job — the Blackwell path works, and the 10.9× isolated speedup they promise is real when you feed it inputs from the distribution their math assumes.

26. Hebbian-MoE Phase 1: dense routing, then a top-2 surprise

2026-07-03 to 2026-07-04

While 500M v2 pretrains on the 5090 (a ~10-day job), the 5080 took on the next unimplemented item from the research agenda: Hebbian Mixture-of-Experts, discussed earlier as "the real DeepSeek-style scaling lever" for this architecture — N parallel Hebbian memory lanes per layer, routed by spike pattern, each keeping its own M-state. The promise is scaling effective params without scaling active compute, the same trade DeepSeek's MoE FFNs make.

Design: reuse the kernel, don't rewrite it

New module MoEHebbianBlock (src/moe_hebbian.py). Key decisions:

  • Shared spiking front-end. One LIF layer produces the spike pattern; the router and every expert read from the same spikes. Avoids a chicken-and-egg problem (routing needs spikes, but per-expert spikes would need a routing decision first) and mirrors how real MoE only replicates the FFN, not the attention/embedding path.
  • Full d_mem per expert, not a split fraction — real per-expert capacity, unlike the existing n_heads mechanism which divides one memory into smaller diagonal blocks.
  • Expert dimension collapsed into the batch dimension for the memory update — exactly the same trick n_heads>1 already used. This meant delta_chunked_gated and the new delta_fla_gated kernel needed zero modification; an N-expert layer is just a (B·N)-batch call to code that already existed.
  • Phase 1 scope, deliberately narrow: dense compute (every expert runs for every token, blended by router weight) rather than sparse gather/scatter. The point was to prove specialization helps before building the much harder systems engineering (per-expert token dispatch with a stateful recurrent memory is harder than standard transformer-FFN MoE, where tokens are independent).

Smoke-tested on CPU and GPU, eager and fla kernel, before any real training: forward/backward clean, gradients reach the router and every expert, no NaN, load-balance aux loss shrinks under training.

First sweep: routers collapse at low N

ConfigN expertsval_pplRouting
control181.20—
moe2286.35collapsed (peak weight 1.00)
moe4492.97collapsed (peak weight 1.00)
moe8877.87partial escape (peak 0.52)

Only N=8 had enough experts to escape the router's basin of attraction at moe_aux_weight: 0.01 — a fully-collapsed router only costs 0.01×(N−1) in loss (0.01 / 0.03 / 0.07 for N=2/4/8), negligible against a CE loss around 9. Diagnosed, not just observed: added per-expert routing-weight and peak-weight logging ([moe] expert_util lines) to every eval, so "balanced load" and "genuine per-token specialization" are distinguishable going forward.

Aux weight fixed it — and surfaced a routing-hardness question

Bumped moe_aux_weight 0.01 → 0.1 and reran. N=4 and N=8 both escaped collapse; N=2 balanced on average (0.49/0.51) but each token still routed ~100% to one expert — not enough experts for real specialization signal to matter.

Configval_ppl (best)Routing
moe2_auxbig81.85–103balanced average, no real per-token spread
moe4_auxbig76.26balanced, peak 0.54 — genuine specialization
moe8_auxbig75.45balanced, peak 0.34 — best dense result

Then tested routing hardness at N=4: top-1 (hard) routing was worse than dense soft blending — 89.43 vs 76.26. Forcing full commitment to one expert loses signal the soft blend was using productively.

Top-2 routing was the standout result of the whole sweep. moe4_top2 (top-2 of 4 experts, aux weight 0.1) reached val_ppl 70.53 — better than every dense config, including N=8. More interesting than the ppl number: ablating memory blew up val_ppl 4–6× (ablated_ppl 270–586 vs val_ppl 70–120 across evals, memory Δ up to +357) — far beyond anything else in the sweep (which topped out around Δ+48). Layer 2's routing also showed a real, stable specialization pattern (expert 0 at 0.5–0.65, expert 1 at 0.03–0.11, consistent across the back half of training). Reading: top-2 sits in a sweet spot — enough hard commitment to force genuinely distinct per-expert memories, enough redundancy (2 picks, not 1) to avoid top-1's collapse-under-noise failure mode.

Given how noisy this sweep has been (single runs swinging 15–20 val_ppl points), a second-seed confirmation run was queued before trusting the result or spending a 90M-scale validation run on it — in progress as this entry is written.

Two more tweaks staged behind the confirmation run

While the seed-1 confirmation cooks, added two more config-gated (default-off) options to MoEHebbianBlock, both smoke-tested (grads flow, no NaN, and — important for anything stochastic — verified off in eval mode):

  • route_noise_std — Shazeer-style Gaussian noise on router logits during training, the standard noisy-top-k exploration trick.
  • router_z_weight — ST-MoE router z-loss, penalizing large router logits to keep the gate softmax numerically calm.

Both queued as 30M screens (seed 0, directly comparable to the 70.53 baseline) to run after the seed-1 confirmation, before committing to the expensive 90M scale-up.

Gotcha: VRAM scales with B×N_experts, not just B

Because the expert dimension collapses into the batch dimension for the memory kernel, an N=8 run at the same batch_size as N=4 pushes 2× the rows through the delta kernel. This caused two near-miss OOM incidents this session (once catastrophic — system-wide VRAM thrash, tok/s crawled to 373 and made the whole PC sluggish; once a slower creep from a stale config comment that no longer matched its own batch_size). Fix in both cases: hold batch_size × n_experts constant (=32) across every config in the sweep, trading batch_size for grad_accum as N grows, so every run in a comparison has the identical memory-kernel footprint and the identical effective batch size.

Where this stood at the time. Phase 1 (dense/soft MoE) showed a real, if noisy, quality signal — and top-2 routing's memory-Δ result looked like the most interesting finding of the sweep. The next section resolves whether it held up.

27. MoE sweep concludes: the top-2 surprise was noise; router z-loss is the real winner. Adaptive computation lands, halting not yet real.

2026-07-04 (night) to 2026-07-05

The top-2 memory-Δ blowup did not reproduce. A second-seed confirmation run (same config, seed: 1) was queued specifically because this sweep had been noisy all along. It came back with val_ppl 76.70 (fine, but well off seed0's 70.53) and — the important part — memory Δ of only +1 to +28, nothing like seed0's +159–357 blowup. Routing was balanced too, none of seed0's starved-expert pattern. The dramatic mechanism was seed luck, not a property of top-2 routing. Good thing it was checked before a 90M-scale run got built around it.

Two more tweaks were screened at N=4 (aux_weight 0.1) to find something that actually holds up:

Noisy top-k (Shazeer): killed mid-run as a lost cause. Best val_ppl only ~87, and ablated_ppl grew unbounded over training (1886 → 5692) even as val_ppl itself declined normally — not a training bug, a real architectural failure mode. Noisy routing is a load-balancing aid, but aux_weight: 0.1 had already solved load balancing (routers were balanced, peak weight 0.70–0.85). Adding noise on top didn't fix a live problem; it randomized which expert got picked often enough that no expert's FF pathway could specialize, so the model dumped everything onto the one reliable signal left — the transient Hebbian memory — and the ablation probe caught it red-handed. Lower σ would only reduce the damage, never flip the sign, so no further noisy screens are planned.

Router z-loss (ST-MoE) is the sweep's real winner: val_ppl 66.00 at 30M — best of the entire sweep, and healthy: routing balanced (peak ~0.70–0.73), memory Δ a sane +17 to +49 (no blowup, no collapse). Applying the seed-noise lesson properly this time, a seed-1 confirmation for z-loss was queued before trusting it or spending GPU-days at 90M scale.

Adaptive computation lands (the other research-agenda item)

Built PonderRefine (src/adaptive_compute.py), a PonderNet-style (Banino et al. 2021) halting head: a shared refinement transform applies to the final hidden state up to ponder_steps times, a halting head produces per-step halt probabilities, and the output is the probability-weighted sum — the model's expected output under its own halting distribution. Phase 1 scope matched the MoE precedent exactly: dense (every step computed for every token, soft-weighted), true dynamic early-exit deferred to Phase 2. Implemented at the LM level, block-type-agnostic — works with plain or MoE blocks with zero changes to either. Smoke-tested: gradients reach both the refine transform and the halt head, ponder-cost pressure demonstrably lowers expected step count, eval mode is deterministic.

30M screen: val_ppl 70.13 (decent), but the halting mechanism itself didn't work. expected_steps hit 3.000 out of a max of 3 from the second eval onward and never moved — the head learned "always use every pass," zero per-token adaptivity. ponder_cost_weight: 0.01 was far too weak against the cross-entropy gradient's pull toward more refinement — the exact same failure shape as the MoE router collapsing at aux_weight: 0.01 before that got bumped to 0.1. So 70.13 is really "a fixed 3-pass refinement MLP helps a little," not adaptive computation yet. Next: re-screen with the cost weight raised substantially (0.1, 0.3) and check whether expected_steps actually varies.

A lesson in premature scaling

Built and VRAM-validated a 90M z-loss config (176M actual params once MoE's expert multiplication is counted, batch=6 for safe headroom), launched it, then killed it after ~5M tokens on realizing two things at once: z-loss hadn't been seed-confirmed yet, and a lone 90M run without a matched 90M baseline wouldn't prove anything even if it looked good. Corrected sequence: confirm the 30M seed first (cheap, ~3h), then if it holds, run a 90M pair (z-loss + baseline, ~300M tokens each rather than the full 1B — enough to see whether the relative edge survives scaling without committing a week of GPU time to it).

Where this stands now. MoE Phase 1's real, reproducible-so-far finding is router z-loss (66.00 at 30M, seed1 confirming). Adaptive computation is built and partially working (quality lift, no real adaptivity yet). Meanwhile 500M v2 passed 1.27B/5B tokens on the 5090, still running clean.

28. MoE Phase 1 closes as a null: both wins failed reseed. Adaptive-compute overshoots. Roadmap reset.

2026-07-05 (afternoon)

Router z-loss did not confirm either. The seed-1 confirmation run (screen_moe4_top2_zloss_seed1_30m, same config as seed0's 66.00) finished at val_ppl 86.31 — worse than plain top-2's own seed1 baseline (76.70), not just unremarkable. Routing/memory metrics stayed healthy (peak weight 0.670, memory Δ +27.99), so this isn't a training bug — the effect just isn't real. Second confirmed instance of the same lesson as the top-2-routing memory-Δ blowup: single-seed results in this sweep (with a measured 15–20 ppl swing across seeds) cannot be trusted, however clean the metrics look.

Verdict on MoE Phase 1: null-to-inconclusive. Full sweep, seed-annotated:

Config (30M)seed0seed1verdict
control N=181.20—single seed
N=4 dense soft, aux=0.176.26—single seed
N=8 dense soft, aux=0.175.45—single seed (best dense)
top-1 hard89.43—worse, dead
top-2, aux=0.170.5376.70did not reproduce
noisy top-k~87—killed (unbounded ablation blowup)
top-2 + z-loss66.0086.31did not reproduce

Both standout results evaporated on reseed. What's left — the dense N=4/N=8 configs at ~75–76 — is single-seed and only 5–6 ppl below control (81.20), while the sweep's seed swing is 15–20 ppl. The effect size is smaller than the noise floor. That's a null-to-inconclusive result, not a "confirm and scale" story.

Decision: close Phase 1. Do NOT scale to 90M. The queued screen_moe4_top2_zloss_90m + matched _baseline_90m pair (~40h total on the 5080) was killed before launch. Also worth noting: Neuro is already sparse via the spiking front-end — MoE's headline sparsity benefit is muted here in the first place. Formally: MoE Phase 1 shelved pending either (a) a decisive multi-seed anchor test showing MoE beats no-MoE above noise, or (b) a new architectural idea worth screening. Phase 2 (true sparse gather/scatter compute) is only worth building on top of a preserved Phase 1 quality win — and there isn't one.

Adaptive computation: the halting knife-edge, from one collapse to the other

Followed up on the earlier "always-max-steps" collapse (ponder_cost_weight: 0.01) with a 10× stronger cost weight, screen_ponder3_cw01_30m (cost_weight = 0.1). It flipped the failure mode:

cw=0.1 overshot to the opposite collapse: always-MIN. By the second eval and every eval after, expected_steps sat at ~1.000/3 — the halting head learned "always halt immediately after step 1," equally non-adaptive. So we now have both endpoints bracketed:
  • cw=0.01 → expected_steps 3.000 (always max, no cost pressure)
  • cw=0.1 → expected_steps 1.000 (all cost pressure, no compute investment)
The sweet spot — where the head actually uses different step counts per token — must lie in between. Next screen: cw=0.03 (log-midpoint of the collapsed endpoints). If that also flips to one end or the other, we'll need to swap the plain mean-steps penalty for PonderNet's proper KL-to-geometric-prior regularizer — not just retune the weight.

The MoE work and the adaptive-compute work share a striking failure mode in common: both are "conditional computation" mechanisms (MoE over width / experts, ponder over depth / steps), and both collapse to a constant when their gating regularizer is off — but by opposite constants depending on regularizer strength. Same knife-edge problem, different axis.

Strategic reset: broader roadmap

Stepped back to evaluate the full enhancement backlog against the breakthrough-LLM goal (faster / smarter-per-param / cheaper / adaptable / memory+plasticity / low-energy / auditable). Verdicts:

  • MTP → self-speculative decoding — heads already trained (Batch A). Wire the inference-side draft+verify loop. Biggest "it's fast" demo lever. Tier 1.
  • PMA (Pre-trained Model Averaging, ByteDance Seed) — model-soup the last N checkpoints at run-end. Near-free quality lift, reduces annealing cost, low risk. Build the averaging script now on existing ckpts; apply at 500M run-end. Tier 1.
  • Engram (DeepSeek n-gram hash → embedding store) — static factual store complementing the dynamic Hebbian fast-weight. Cheap recall (no matmul), low energy, and literally auditable (an inspectable dictionary). Serves several breakthrough pillars at once. Tier 1, on-theme.
  • Adaptive-compute halting fix ("looped transformers") — the ponder work above; finish the cost-weight/regularizer tuning to make halting genuinely per-token. Tier 2, in flight.
  • Brain-mod improvements mined from LoRA/DoRA — low-rank / magnitude-decomposition / composition tricks to sharpen Neuro's existing PEFT differentiator. Tier 2.
  • Deferred / research-only: RLM (context-as-environment, app-layer, overlaps split-replay), CTM (high-risk rebuild vs current LIF+recurrent for unclear gain), World models (multi-year scope; MTP+self-refine already give lightweight lookahead), Diffusion LLM (a different model, not an enhancement — separate track tied to future multimodal).
Underexploited pillar: auditability. Engram (inspectable fact table) + spiking sparsity (sparse activation traces) + brain mods (modular capability units) together form Neuro's real auditability story — a genuine differentiator against black-box dense transformers. Worth leaning into for the narrative.

What's running now

500M v2 pretrain still healthy on the 5090 (past 1.29B/5B tokens, val_ppl 18.02, ~5,800 tok/s). 5080 pivoting from MoE closure to the adaptive-compute cw=0.03 probe.

29. Engram screened (didn't confirm), adaptive-compute rebuilt twice, and the recurrent-depth rewrite

2026-07-05 (evening)

A dense session on two Tier-1 roadmap items — Engram (a static factual-recall store) and adaptive computation — with the discipline learned from MoE (never trust a single seed) applied throughout, plus a full architectural rewrite once the literature made clear our first design was structurally wrong.

Engram: mechanism works, quality win doesn't reproduce

Built src/engram.py — a DeepSeek-style n-gram hash store: a multiplicative rolling hash over the last 3 tokens indexes one of 32,768 buckets, whose low-rank vector projects to a vocab logit bias, added in parallel with the Markov head. ~3.1M params. The pitch: matmul-free factual recall (faster/cheaper/lower-energy) and literal auditability (an inspectable dictionary), forming a "two memory systems" story alongside the dynamic Hebbian fast-weight.

Two seeds, no confirmed win. seed0 ended at val_ppl 75.66 (beating control's 81.20); seed1 ended at 87.13 (above it). An 11+ ppl spread, and seed1 doesn't beat control — the seed0 result was noise, exactly the MoE lesson again. The memory-Δ stayed large and positive across both seeds (+13 to +74), so the Engram pathway is actively contributing to predictions; it just doesn't net-improve perplexity robustly at this config/scale. Banked as inconclusive-to-negative; not promoted. The auditability/energy story stays attractive if a config that actually improves quality can be found later.

Adaptive computation, take 2: MoR expert-choice — collapse-proof, but memory-fragile

After the PonderNet halting head collapsed (§28), rebuilt adaptive-compute around Mixture-of-Recursions expert-choice routing (Bae et al., Jul 2025): a hard top-k capacity per depth, so exactly a fixed fraction of tokens continue each step and collapse is structurally impossible. It worked — genuine, non-degenerate per-token depth, no collapse.

But the memory-ablation Δ grew without bound (+205 → +968 and still climbing over training, vs. +tens for every other config at this recipe). Researched it: the module was a separate learned MLP bolted onto the final hidden state, disconnected from the Hebbian memory. Discrete top-k is fragile at its decision boundary (our depth histogram polarized over training, confirming it), and the gate conflated routing with refinement magnitude. Two independent papers explain the fix — and both say the same thing.

The rewrite: loop the actual Hebbian block (recurrent depth)

Three 2026 papers converge on how adaptive depth should really be built: Geiping/Huginn (loop the model's real core block in latent space with input injection, not a bolt-on), STARS (stabilize the loop as a contraction), and — most pointedly — "Universal Transformers Need Memory" (when a model already has recurrent memory, the recursion must share it, not run a separate module). Our bolt-on violated exactly this, which is why ablation broke it: a module outside the memory computation develops its own fragile memory-dependence, whereas the Hebbian blocks degrade gracefully because memory-ablation has been a normal eval mode for them since day one.

Rewrote adaptive-compute to loop the actual SpikingHebbianBlock stack in latent space, sharing its memory. Input injection each pass (re-add the embedding — Huginn's gradient-flow trick), fresh memory rebuilt per pass (shares the memory machinery without double-writing), truncated-BPTT option for scalability. Gated so recurrent_depth=1 is an exact no-op (verified 0.00e+00 logit diff) — zero regression for every existing config, and the existing control (81.20) already is the depth-1 result. Verified R=2 trains cleanly with a bounded ablation-Δ (+0.36 CE on toy data) — the whole point, vs. the bolt-on's runaway +968.

Staged to fail cheap: Stage 1 (screening now, screen_rdepth2_30m) tests only the load-bearing hypothesis — does fixed depth-2 beat depth-1 (81.20) with bounded ablation-Δ? If yes, Stage 2 adds the MoR per-token capacity router for adaptive depth plus depth-sampled training for test-time scalability. If the Δ is bounded but there's no quality gain, depth-2 alone isn't enough and we rethink. Either way it's a clean, cheap signal — the right way to de-risk a core-forward-path change.

Where this stands. Engram: banked, didn't confirm. Adaptive-compute: the fragile bolt-on is superseded; the literature-optimal recurrent-depth design (loop the real Hebbian block, share memory) is built, verified as a no-op at R=1 and stable at R=2, and Stage-1 screening is underway. 500M v2 pretraining continued clean throughout (past 1.46B/5B tokens, val_ppl ~17.9).

30. Recurrent-depth wins at 200M tokens; 500M v2 throughput swap (+28%); router quality-gradient fix

2026-07-06

Three interleaving threads landed in a single working session: Stage-1 recurrent-depth resolved into a real quality result (with a diagnostic detour), 500M v2 pretraining picked up a ~28% throughput lift from a one-restart swap, and a code review of the previous session's commits exposed a design flaw in the Stage-2 router that got quietly fixed before it could contaminate the next screen.

Stage 1 at 100M: qualified pass, quality miss — and the token-budget hypothesis

screen_rdepth2_30m finished at val_ppl 91.59 vs control 81.20 — on face value a failure of criterion (a). But the eval curve was still falling steeply at budget end (180 → 137 → 118 → 98 → 96 → 91.6) while sibling configs at this recipe plateau by then, and memory-Δ was bounded and declining (+304 → +119), exactly the opposite shape of the MoR-bolt-on blowup. Structure sound, quality just under-trained — a known Geiping/Huginn signature (looped models converge slower per token but may have a higher ceiling). Instead of banking as a null, we tested the token-budget hypothesis directly with a fresh screen_rdepth2_200mtok_30m.

Depth-2 at 200M WON: val_ppl 75.91 vs control 81.20 (−5.3, −6.5%), memory-Δ bounded in the tens throughout (+72 → +49 → +36 → +49 → +59 → +42 → +43 → +32 → +40 — healthy oscillation, not the runaway blowup that killed the bolt-on). This is the first real quality-win of the whole recurrent-depth arc, and validates the Geiping/Huginn "loop the real block, share the memory" thesis for this architecture. Seed-1 confirmation is running now — same MoE/Engram discipline that caught two prior single-seed near-misses.

A depth-3 fallback (screen_rdepth3_30m, 100M tokens) ran in parallel and finished at val_ppl 89.13 with elevated but declining Δ (+446 → +103). Curve shape mirrors depth-2 @ 100M's under-training profile — no verdict on depth-3 at parity, needs its own 200M screen after Stage 2 resolves.

Code review found and fixed a design flaw in the Stage-2 router

Between finishing Stage-1 build and launching Stage 2, a review of the last 24h of commits flagged three real bugs (all in the recurrent-depth code path) and one open design gap. The bugs got fixed same session:

  • Recurrent branch discarded its Hebbian memory states → return_final_state returned all-zeros → brain snapshots for all rdepth runs were silently garbage. Would have broken split-replay / stateful inference on any recurrent-depth model.
  • ~128 MB / forward of unused zero-initialized state allocation in the recurrent branch.
  • recurrent_depth > 1 combined with stateful_stream silently bypassed the recurrent loop (the initial_state path is single-pass). Now raises loudly.
The design gap: RecurrentDepthRouter.gate() used a hard torch.where(keep, h_new, h_old) merge, and its aux BCE loss only calibrated the router score against its own top-k membership — self-referential. No gradient path from the CE loss to the routing scores. The router would have learned consistency, not correctness: which tokens deserved more depth was never optimized, only self-reinforced from initial ordering. Deliberately left in place until confirmed by seed-1's verdict on whether Stage 2 was worth launching at all — then fixed via MoR's "select AND scale" prescription: kept tokens now merge as h_old + g·(h_new − h_old) at train time, so CE gradients flow through g into the routing scores. Eval preserves the hard select (honest val_ppl). Smoke-tested: the router now receives real CE gradient with the aux BCE weight zeroed — before the fix that gradient was strictly zero.

500M v2 throughput audit & the compile-mode footgun

With Stage-1 scoping done, an audit of the 500M v2 pretraining loop identified the KD teacher (Phi-3.5-mini, 3.8B, running full fwd every microstep with no compile) as ~70% of theoretical step FLOPs and the dominant lever. Two independent tweaks prepped: torch.compile the teacher, and re-split the batch from 6×5 (eff 30) to 8×4 (eff 32) — fewer, larger microsteps.

First swap attempt burned an hour. With compile_mode: reduce-overhead, VRAM pinned at 32032/32607 MiB and the CUDA driver silently paged ~1.8 GB to shared/host memory over PCIe every step. Threshold delivered 4,475 tok/s vs the 5,841 baseline — worse. Root cause: reduce-overhead's CUDA-graph replay preallocates the peak, and combined with the teacher's forward buffers + batch 8, we crossed the 32 GB boundary. New gotcha banked: on Blackwell 32 GB with a large compiled teacher, reduce-overhead is untenable at these batch sizes.
Swap succeeded with compile_mode: default (fuses without CUDA-graph replay). Steady-state 7,352–7,598 tok/s at step 112200–300 — ~+28% end-to-end, VRAM stable at 25.6 GB with 6 GB headroom. Loss trajectory clean, first eval post-swap gave val_ppl 14.84 (in-line with pre-swap curve). Remaining schedule: ~6.5 days → ~5.1 days — ~1.4 days saved. Rollback preserved (the pre-swap ckpt dir is untouched).

A follow-up: tried kd_teacher_int8 via bitsandbytes for another ~15-25% (memory-lever). Bnb 0.49.2 loads on Blackwell sm_120 cleanly, val_ppl stayed fine after the swap — but tok/s dropped from ~7,500 back to ~5,900. On Blackwell the bf16 tensor cores are so fast that bnb's Linear8bitLt dequant-per-matmul path (fp16 compute internally, with a graph-break for the compiled teacher) is slower than the direct bf16 matmul. int8 wins are memory-bound territory; we're compute-bound here. Reverted the flag but kept the code path — useful for future larger teachers where memory is the binder.

Where this stood at end of session

500M v2 running at ~+28% on the 5090, val_ppl declining, memory-Δ high (which in pretraining means the model uses its Hebbian memory — the opposite semantic from the 30M screens where high Δ meant fragility). Seed-1 confirm of depth-2 @ 200M running on the 5080.
