63. The post-training stack grows a third stage, tool calling and two auxiliary heads; a silent vocab bug that was corrupting every tool target; and the first MFU measurement this project has ever taken

2026-09-20

§62 closed with post-training “recipe-complete” at SFT → ORPO, gated only on the base. A research sweep of 2026 literature reopened it: Glenn picked four ideas off a shortlist — on-policy distillation, tool calling, why-narrated alignment, calibrated abstention — and two more (a confidence head, an energy/verifier head) as stretch. All six got built today, and the throughput investigation that followed produced the more consequential finding.

The stack is now SFT → ORPO → OPD

On-policy distillation (src/train_opd.py) is the stage Qwen3, MiMo, GLM-5 and MiniCPM5 all converged on in 2026: the student samples its own trajectories and a frozen teacher scores every token, giving RL's on-policy state distribution with distillation's dense per-token supervision. Three design points were forced by this architecture rather than chosen. The teacher must be a Llama-3-tokenizer model, because our 32768 vocab is a LUT projection of Llama-3's 128256 — and the LUT turns out to be injective on its nonzero values (32767 entries, 32767 distinct), so inv[student_id] aligns the two sequences position-for-position and id 0 is the only many-to-one slot. The loss is a support-restricted reverse KL over the teacher's top-K plus a -log(student mass inside that support) term — not a hack, since in true reverse KL the outside-support region is where the teacher's probability is ~0 and therefore dominates. And rollouts use full-prefix re-forward, not incremental initial_state decoding, because src/hybrid_attn.py keeps no KV cache: single-token stepping would show the attention layer a length-1 sequence and produce logits that differ from the ones the loss differentiates, making the rollouts quietly off-policy.

Tool calling (“cognitive core”) got a locked protocol — TOOL {json} or ANSWER <text>, arguments in schema order, a 9-tool registry in training_data/posttrain/tools.json — and a 501-node character DFA for grammar-constrained decoding (src/grammar.py). At the first response position it admits 5 of 32768 tokens. It rejects a wrong tool name, wrong argument order, a number passed as a string, and any tool absent from the offered list. Why-narrated ORPO and calibrated abstention are data-side: the generator emits why_vs_rejected and why_vs_bare pairs (the second isolates “state the reason” from “be correct”), and abstention pairs in both directions, since training only the unanswerable half teaches a model to always abstain. The two auxiliary heads (src/heads.py) read hidden states through a forward pre-hook on model.head — no src/model.py edit, verified by model.head(captured) reproducing the logits at 0.0 max difference.

The encoder was silently deleting words, and would have corrupted the entire tool dataset. train_sft.load_lut_encode drops any Llama-3 token whose LUT entry is 0 — and 74.5% of Llama-3's vocab maps to 0. So "calculator" encoded to "", "kilometers" to "ometers", "celsius" to "c", ANSWER to ANS, and ", "arguments": { to ", "": {. Nothing warned; the targets just quietly lost their content words. Caught only because a round-trip assertion in a unit test failed. Fix: src/lut_spell.py keeps the pretraining tokenization for tokens the student has and re-spells the OOV ones out of in-vocab subwords (greedy longest match, ~5400 calls/s) — 100% round-trip on the whole protocol, 0 unspellable characters except genuine non-ASCII like ∨. Opt-in via lut_subword_fallback, default off, so existing runs stay byte-identical. Same bug class as the two build-drift fixes: a shared helper quietly doing the wrong thing for a new stage.

Teacher data generation ran on the 5080 with Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct: tools 24,000, abstain 16,000, coldstart 16,000 complete; why deferred at 128. Getting there cost four restarts and taught the sizing lesson properly. Per-target rates span ~30× — tools ~750 records/min, abstain ~1290, coldstart ~70, why ~40 (a whole reply sits in its prompt) — so a batch benchmarked on one target means nothing on another, and an uncapped slow target starves everything queued behind it. The unobvious VRAM hog is the prefill logits tensor: HF computes logits for every prompt position on the first forward, which at a 128k teacher vocab is ~2.5GB at batch 64. On coldstart, batch 128 failed outright and batch 64 crawled at 28 records/min at 15.9/16.3 GB — spilling to WDDM — while batch 32 ran 2.5× faster at 10.1 GB. Staying in physical VRAM beats a bigger batch, the same conclusion the 500M-era sizing work reached.

The OPD GPU smoke passed on a real 363M student against the real 3B teacher. Ten steps: rkl 2.67→1.97, mass_in 0.081→0.104, no_sup 0.000 throughout — the teacher's top-16 always lands inside our 32k vocab, which was the main unvalidated assumption in the whole stage. Zero skipped microsteps. 13.7/14.8 GB, confirming OPD belongs on the 5090.

But throughput was 14–17 tok/s, which makes the configured 8M-token budget a six-day run. The cause is the full-prefix re-forward: 32 forwards per step where one would do. The fix is the one hybrid_attn.py already names in its own docstring — add step() with a growing KV cache — and it would pay off in six places at once: OPD training, tool_eval, abstain_eval, head_eval, self_refine, and the TTFT-vs-Transformer benchmark that has been on the to-do list since §61.

flagship_v4: the first MFU number, and it reorders the plan

Glenn's next goal is a 1B/20B-token flagship_v4 trained in under 30 days. That question turned out to be unanswerable, because nothing in this repo had ever measured MFU — the §63-era speed work concluded “overhead-bound, not FLOP-bound” from wall-clock deltas alone. So scripts/mfu_report.py now measures FLOPs exactly with torch.utils.flop_counter.FlopCounterMode over a real forward+backward, rather than applying the 6N rule (which is off by 1.15× here).

flagship_v3 runs at 27.3% MFU. 426.5M params, 2.95 GFLOP/token measured, 28.6 TFLOPS achieved against the 5090's ~104.8 TFLOPS BF16-dense/FP32-accumulate ceiling. 10B tokens at that rate is 11.9 days, matching the observed ~13 with eval overhead. For reference, LLMQ (arXiv:2512.15306) demonstrates 61–70% MFU on consumer cards — so there is real headroom, not a wall.

The per-component breakdown redirected the whole plan. The three GDN-2 gate projections — W_fdecay, W_w, W_b, each a full Linear(n_neurons=3840 → d_mem=1280) — are together ~25% of all training FLOPs, while the entire attention layer is 1.8% (one layer in nine). So FlashAttention-class optimization is irrelevant to our throughput, and the expensive thing is a gate. An earlier read of the same data said 50%; that was wrong — the aggregation counted parent and child modules both, inflating leaf shares ~2.07×. The corrected 25% is confirmed by the measured saving matching it almost exactly.

GLA (arXiv:2312.06635) parameterizes precisely this gate as a rank-16 bottleneck, explicitly because the full mapping is “prohibitively expensive”, and ablates it as sufficient: 14.77 ppl at rank-16 vs 15.56 for data-independent scalar decay vs 23.21 for no gate. The gate must be data-dependent; it does not need full rank. Ours was the most expensive possible choice. gate_rank now builds a LowRankLinear bottleneck for the three gates, exposing .weight/.bias as properties onto the up-factor so the forward pass, the W_fdecay slow-decay init (.weight.mul_(0.01), .bias.fill_(-3.0)) and muon.py's routing all work untouched; gate_rank: 0 is the default and byte-identical.

gate_rankparamsFLOPs/tokenvs baseline
0 (current)426.5M2.95 G—
32312.4M2.27 G−27% params, −23% FLOPs

Quality is unscreened — a 2-seed 30M A/B over gate_rank ∈ {0, 16, 32} is the gate on this lever. The −27% params is a second-order win that matters for v4 specifically: it frees VRAM, which buys batch, which buys utilization, and 1B on a 32GB card is tight. Stacked with FP8 (1.25–1.4×, Blackwell-native; the earlier “Blackwell int8 regression” note was about inference int8 and does not apply) and CUDA graphs (1.15–1.3×, still blocked on the §58 MQAR restructure), the compounded projection is ~2.1× — past the 1.85× that 1B/20B-in-30-days needs.

On data: the 20B-token budget exceeds our 14.53B corpus, but the source pools (~79.5B tokens across DCLM, FineWeb-Edu, fine-pdfs, Cosmopedia, OpenMath, StarCoder, FineWiki) are far larger than what was sampled — they are simply not on disk, having been streamed at build time. The mix is also 38% raw DCLM against 22% FineWeb-Edu despite 9.68B available and only 3.30B used, and FineWeb-Edu's educational classifier is worth 4–6 MMLU points over raw Common Crawl at fixed compute. So the lever is curation, not acquisition: rebalance toward the high-quality pools, then a DoReMi-style proxy sweep on the existing 30M/90M screen harness (reported 2.6× fewer steps to baseline accuracy) to set the weights properly.

Major version: 1.48 (Post-training extended to a THIRD stage: src/train_opd.py = on-policy distillation [support-restricted reverse KL + out-of-support mass penalty; teacher MUST be Llama-3-tokenizer since vocab_map_32768 is INJECTIVE on nonzero -> position-for-position alignment; full-prefix re-forward rollouts because hybrid_attn keeps no KV cache]. GPU smoke PASSED on real 363M+3B: rkl 2.67->1.97, no_sup 0.000, 0 skipped, 13.7/14.8GB -> OPD is 5090-only. BUT 14-17 tok/s = 6d for 8M tok; fix = KV cache in hybrid_attn [pays off in OPD + tool_eval + abstain_eval + head_eval + self_refine + TTFT bench]. Also built: tool protocol TOOL/ANSWER + 501-node DFA constrained decoding [5 of 32768 tokens legal at position 0], why-narrated + both-direction abstention pairs, confidence + energy heads via a forward pre-hook on model.head [no model.py edit]. CRITICAL BUG: load_lut_encode DELETES Llama-3 tokens outside the 32k vocab [74.5% of it] -> "calculator"->"", "kilometers"->"ometers"; fix src/lut_spell.py re-spells from in-vocab subwords, opt-in lut_subword_fallback. Data on 5080: tools 24k + abstain 16k + coldstart 16k DONE, why deferred at 128; per-target rates span 30x and the prefill-logits tensor [batch x prompt_len x 128256 x 2B] sets the batch ceiling -- coldstart batch 32 is 2.5x FASTER than 64 because 64 spills to WDDM. flagship_v4 throughput: FIRST EVER MFU measurement via scripts/mfu_report.py = 27.3% [2.95 GFLOP/token, 28.6 of 104.8 TFLOPS]; the 3 GDN-2 gates are ~25% of FLOPs and ALL attention is 1.8%; gate_rank low-rank gates [GLA rank-16 precedent] give -27% params/-23% FLOPs at rank 32, default 0 = byte-identical, quality UNSCREENED. See neuro_posttrain_v3_stack_built_2026_09_20 + neuro_lut_oov_drop_bug + neuro_v4_throughput_mfu_2026_09_20 + reference_teacher_gen_5080 + HANDOFF_2026_09_20b.)

64. gate_rank confirmed a real win, the KV-cache path parked on a token-decomposability wall, and the 5080's 16GB proves the recurring ceiling for scale work

2026-09-22

§63 left three threads open: adopt gate_rank pending a screen, build the KV cache hybrid_attn.py keeps asking for, and run the post-training stack. This session closed the first, hit a wall on the second, and discovered a hardware ceiling that reshapes the third — plus finished the last teacher dataset.

gate_rank is a screened win, and it's real. The 2-seed 30M A/B over {0, 16, 32} came back with low-rank gates beating full-rank, not just tying: val_ppl 2-seed mean r0 72.0 / r16 60.6 (−16%) / r32 68.8, with split-replay recall holding at every N (r16/r32 hit 100% at N=16 where the control sags to ~92%). The rank-16 bottleneck regularizes gates that otherwise overfit — exactly GLA's finding. Because low-rank beating full-rank is surprising, I re-ran ppl_eval.py (trainer's seed + val split) out-of-loop on the saved checkpoints: r0 71.68 / r16 60.88, reproducing the gap. Not a logging artifact. Decision: adopt gate_rank:16 for v4; staged into configs/flagship_v4_363m.draft.yaml.

The KV cache went further than §63's one-line framing and then hit a wall. The attention primitive built cleanly: HybridAttentionBlock.step_kv with a growing (k,v) cache, parity-tested to 3.6e-7 against forward_sequence — after catching that SDPA's is_causal=True aligns the causal mask top-left, so a cached single query would see only key 0; the fix is an explicit bottom-right mask. Threaded through model.forward behind a kv_cache arg, gated so training stays byte-identical.

The model is not token-decomposable, and attention was never the only blocker. A model-level parity test failed by ~5.7. Isolating it: a pure-spiking stack, fed token-by-token via initial_state, diverges from a full forward by ~3.2 — even the 2-pass split does. Root cause: forward_sequence computes the LIF membrane via lif_parallel, which starts its scan from zero every call and never accepts the incoming mem. Only M and prev_spk are threaded; the membrane is dropped. So exact incremental decode needs threading the membrane (and the boundary prev-spike) through lif_parallel/_scan_chunked — a change to the core spiking primitive the live pretrain imports, not a bolt-on KV cache. The §63 handoff's “attention was the only layer that couldn't decode incrementally” was wrong. Split-replay still works because build-then-query never claimed equivalence to a single pass. PARKED until the base hits 10B; scope via /new-feature. The attention foundation stays in place, gated and inert.

The 5080's 16GB is the recurring ceiling for anything at 90M or 363M scale. Two attempts hit the same wall. Full-parameter 363M/426M SFT/ORPO OOMs at any batch — the fixed footprint (params + NorMuon optimizer state + grads + CUDA context) is ~15GB, so batch 4/2/1 all sit at 14.6–15.9GB and spill/OOM at the first eval; run_v3_posttrain.sh's GPU=1 SKIP_OPD=1 “5080 does SFT+ORPO” was never actually viable. And the 90M gate_rank confirm: the 16GB caps batch at 16 (24 spills to WDDM), leaving the GPU ~11% utilized at 4.3k tok/s, so a 3h window bought only 20M tokens/arm — at which the models are undertrained and val_ppl ties (r0 60.42 / r16 60.60), the early-training pattern before the gate gap develops. Inconclusive, not a refutation. Real scale confirms belong on the 5090. Saved screen_gr90_* ckpts can resume to a fuller budget.

With the post-training screen blocked on the 5080, the overnight window went to the one thing that fit: finishing the why teacher data. It surfaced a quiet bug — the run why call had been deleted from run_5080_posttrain_gen.sh when why was deferred in §63, so the chain skipped straight to END. Restored it (batch 16, ~8.6GB); why completed at 6,000 records, so all four teacher datasets are now done. The final ORPO no longer runs on a 128-record why stub. A LoRA post-training screen was assessed for the 5080 (fits easily; useful for pipeline de-risking — tool grammar, abstain metric, ORPO stability on the real 426M backbone — but not for final quality, since LoRA-at-363M is unvalidated and the release uses full FT) and deferred to its own window.

One process fix, prompted by a near-miss: a research sweep this session surfaced six post-training and eight pretraining ideas (HOLA, Sparse Delta Memory, metaplastic write gate, any-order objective, LLC/J-lens diagnostics, Muon-Hyperball/KL-SOAP, KV compression) that existed only in chat and would have been lost. All 14 are now in the to-do backlog with status tags, and the “capture ideas immediately” rule is enforced in three places — ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md, handoff Step 2, and feedback_capture_ideas.

Major version: 1.49 (gate_rank SCREENED A WIN at 30M: 2-seed val_ppl r0 72.0 / r16 60.6 [-16%] / r32 68.8, recall holds; verified out-of-loop via ppl_eval [r0 71.68 / r16 60.88] -> adopt gate_rank:16, staged in configs/flagship_v4_363m.draft.yaml. KV-cache incremental decode: attention step_kv built + parity 3.6e-7 [SDPA is_causal is top-left-aligned = wrong for cache; use explicit mask] + threaded through model.forward gated, BUT model is NOT token-decomposable [lif_parallel resets LIF membrane each call; M/prev_spk threaded, mem dropped] -> needs a core-primitive change, PARKED until base 10B, scope via /new-feature. 5080 16GB = recurring ceiling: full-param 363M post-training OOMs at any batch [~15GB fixed]; 90M gate_rank confirm INCONCLUSIVE [batch capped 16 -> 11% util -> 4.3k tok/s -> only 20M tok/arm -> undertrained -> tied], not a refutation. why teacher data COMPLETE at 6000 [run why line had been dropped from the gen script; restored] -> all 4 datasets done. 14 research ideas [6 post-training + 8 pretraining] captured to to-do backlog; idea-capture rule enforced in CLAUDE.md + handoff + feedback_capture_ideas. See neuro_v4_throughput_mfu_2026_09_20 + neuro_kv_cache_not_decomposable + neuro_posttrain_v3_stack_built_2026_09_20 + HANDOFF_2026_09_22.)

65. Two overnight screens settle two v4 levers — width is a real recall win, block_size stays at 512 — and the post-training pipeline is verified ready to fire

2026-09-25

With flagship_v3 grinding out its last billion tokens on the 5090 and the 5080 free for two nights, we spent the window on the two flagship_v4 levers that need no new code — just config knobs on the proven 30M screen harness from the gate_rank chapter. Both ran as 2-seed, one-variable screens holding gate_rank:16 (the adopted v4 lever), each with a shell-only watchdog that kills a hung stage and lets the resumable orchestrator recover.

Width is a real recall lever, and it hadn't plateaued. Holding gate_rank:16 and d_mem:1024, sweeping n_neurons 1024→1536→2048 moved val_ppl 60.6→56.5→55.8 and recall monotonically at every high N — N32 57→68→72, N64 26→33→44% — with the two seeds landing on top of each other (n2048: 44/44). No sign of a plateau at 2.0×. This is the direct confirmation, at the v4-forward recipe, of the standing memory rule that width (not d_mem, not state) is what buys this architecture's one weak axis. n1536 already captures most of the val_ppl gain; n2048 is where the recall keeps climbing.

The block_size screen came back the other way: at matched tokens/step (bs1024 run at batch 16 vs bs512 at batch 32, since bs1024 at batch 32 would OOM the 5080's 16GB), block_size 512→1024 made val_ppl ~9% worse (55.8→60.9) and recall mixed (N32 72→78 up, N64 44→42 flat). Verdict: keep 512 for v4. Two caveats keep it from being a hard negative — the batch had to be halved to fit, so bs1024 also carried noisier gradients, and the 30M config has no attention layer, which is exactly where block_size would matter most on the 9-layer flagship. Not worth a cleaner re-test unless v4 shows a context-length need. A useful by-product: the bs512 + n2048 arm reproduced the width n2048 result exactly (55.8 val_ppl, 72/44 recall), an independent confirmation of the win above.

With the screens spending the GPU, the rest of the window went to making the imminent post-training launch boringly safe. flagship_v3 is at ~9.84B/10B tokens and lands within a day; the moment it does, GPU=0 bash temp/run_v3_posttrain.sh runs SFT→ORPO→OPD on the 5090. We verified the one thing that could silently corrupt it — lut_subword_fallback: true is present in sft_v3_363m.yaml, so the tool-target vocab bug stays fixed — and confirmed the launcher's hard base-guard (refuses < 9.5B tokens), its own fresh logs/v3_posttrain.done.d markers, and per-stage resume. The one documented open risk, an OPD GPU smoke, turned out to have already run on 09-20 (logs/opd_gpu_smoke.log: 363.5M student + Llama-3.2-3B teacher, 10 clean steps, 0 skipped); the “still needed” to-do note was stale and is now corrected. An audit-config pass on the v4 draft against the live base came back clean — no arch drift, every standing recipe default present.

Finally, the width win pointed at a concrete v4 sizing question: how wide can we go? A build-only param probe (holding gate_rank:16) measured 3840=310M, 4480 [3.5×d]=343M, 5120 [4.0×d]=377M, 5760 [4.5×d]=410M — every candidate below v3's 426M (no gate_rank) that already trains at B=18 under the ~30GB WDDM cliff. So gate_rank freed enough budget that widening to 4.5×d is param-affordable; the only open question is activation VRAM at batch 18, which the 5080 can't answer (363M at B=18 won't fit 16GB). Ready configs temp/probe_b18_w{5760,5120,4480}.yaml settle it on the 5090, widest-first, once the post-training run frees the card — take the largest width that fits.

Major version: 1.50 (Two 5080 overnight screens settled two v4 levers. WIDTH re-probe = SCREENED WIN: n_neurons 1024->1536->2048 [gate_rank:16, d_mem 1024 held] monotonic on val_ppl 60.6->56.5->55.8 AND recall N32 57->68->72 / N64 26->33->44%, seeds tight, no plateau at 2.0x -> widen v4. BLOCK_SIZE 512->1024 = SCREENED KEEP 512: bs1024@b16 val_ppl ~9% worse [55.8->60.9], recall mixed [N32 up N64 flat]; caveats = batch halved to fit 16GB + 30M has no attention layer. bs512+n2048 reproduced width n2048 EXACTLY [55.8, 72/44]. Post-training VERIFIED READY on 5090 when v3 hits 10B [~09-25]: lut_subword_fallback:true present in sft config, base-guard + fresh markers + per-stage resume checked, OPD GPU-smoke ALREADY DONE 09-20 [stale to-do note fixed], audit-config on v4 draft CLEAN. v4 WIDTH PARAM probe [gate_rank:16]: 3840=310M/4480=343M/5120=377M/5760[4.5x]=410M, ALL < v3's 426M-that-fit -> widening to 4.5xd is param-affordable; batch-18 activation-VRAM probe PENDING on 5090 [temp/probe_b18_w*.yaml, widest-first]. See neuro_width_reprobe_launched_2026_09_22 + neuro_posttrain_ready_bs_screen_2026_09_24 + HANDOFF_2026_09_25.)

66. v3 lands at 10B, a code review finds a residual bug that invalidates our headline comparison, post-training survives an OOM, and a launch plan takes shape

2026-09-25 (session b)

flagship_v3 finished cleanly at 10.0B tokens (step_0271268.pt, val_ppl 17.57, its best), and v4 got a concrete 1B target shape. Exact CPU builds put A′ = d2048 / 9 layers / n_neurons 9728 (4.75×d) at 1000.0M. A 30M depth-vs-width screen at matched params (L4 d832 vs the L2 d1024 control from the width chapter) showed the deeper model training 1.8× slower (10.7k vs 18.9k tok/s), which favours staying wide.

The headline comparison was wrong. A careful code review found that HybridAttentionBlock already returns x + attn + ffn and the LM loop adds x again, so every attention layer doubles the residual stream (verified numerically: 44 → 89). v3 has one attention layer and adapted to it, but the 14-layer matched-transformer baseline grew its stream 214×. That handicapped it, so “spiking beats transformer by 34%” is invalid until re-run. It is also the likely true cause of the final_norm requirement for attention stacks.

The review also found that StreamText.batch draws all B windows from one ~37k-token region (correlated, overlapping microbatches), and that the GDN-2 gate telemetry ran every training forward, costing ~14 host syncs and 3 graph breaks per block. We shipped three default-off flags, attn_single_residual, telemetry_eval_only and data_independent_windows, with a parity test showing byte-identical defaults (546968f); the first two are on in the v4 draft. A 30M independent-windows screen is queued. Further throughput candidates (lif_parallel's O(T²) scan with per-iteration syncs, Liger fused-linear CE, pinned H2D copies) wait on a first-ever profile. A cleanup moved 494GB of superseded checkpoints, configs and scripts to a trash dir (2fa039e).

Post-training's first launch OOM'd within minutes. SFT pads each batch to its longest example, so T is ragged. _gdn2_dense_chunk's tail path materialized a (B,C,C,K) fp32 tensor. That was safe when chunks were 64, but at v3's chunk 256 it is ~1.3GB per call. The fix zero-pads the tail to a multiple of the 16-token sub-block (exact no-op rows; parity vs gdn2_sequential ~1e-5 incl. grads, bcf7de7). SFT then ran at ~2.1k tok/s. OPD's 8M-token budget measured out at ~6 days (15 resp tok/s, full-prefix rollouts), so it was cut to 1M (~19h).

Research this session went to the backlog: Google's Dream-RSI (it improves an exploration policy by replaying discovery trees and trains no weights; parked as a research-process idea), “LMs Need Sleep” (consolidate fast-weight memory into slow weights, the closest fit to our Hebbian state), on-policy self-distillation, and Distilled RL. We also set a go-to-market strategy: publish results now, keep the recipe private until v4, open the architecture after. Glenn is founder / Chief Scientist and his son is the intended CEO and public face; brains are to be tested as the unique selling point.

Major version: 1.51 (v3 DONE 10.0B, step_0271268, val_ppl 17.57. v4 shape A' d2048/9L/N9728 = 1000.0M; 30M depth screen: L4 1.8x slower at matched params. CODE REVIEW: attention DOUBLE-RESIDUAL [2x+attn+ffn] -> transformer baseline + 34% claim INVALID; correlated-batch sampler; telemetry host-syncs/graph breaks -> flags attn_single_residual / telemetry_eval_only / data_independent_windows [default off, parity-tested, 546968f]. SFT OOM on GDN-2 ragged tail [(B,C,C,K) at chunk 256] -> zero-pad fix bcf7de7. OPD 8M->1M [6d->19h]. 494GB moved to trash. Backlog: Dream-RSI, Sleep consolidation, OPSD, Distilled RL. Launch/funding/team plan in to-do. See neuro_code_review_2026_09_25 + user_glenn_profile_and_venture + HANDOFF_2026_09_25b.)

67. SFT quietly erased v3’s recall, a rehearsal fix brings it back, five eval bugs fall out of the investigation — and a fair transformer comparison says we win on samples, not on compute

2026-09-26 (overnight + day)

SFT wiped out most of v3’s recall. Single-pass MQAR among-candidates on the 10B base is 100/100/100/72/38% at N=4/8/16/32/64; the finished sft_v3_363m (60M tok) scored 62/69/22/0/0. We paused the pipeline before ORPO. A 6M-token A/B from the base reproduced the drop and located it: the v3 mix (tools+coldstart+alpaca/2) gave 100/100/97/34/3 and alpaca-only gave 100/100/97/34/6 — so it is SFT itself eroding high-N recall, not the tool data. (The v1 “SFT keeps recall” belief in §60 was read through split-replay, see below.)

MQAR rehearsal during SFT restores it, cheaply at low weight. We added mqar_aug to train_sft (the pretraining MQAR aux, default off; smoke-tested) and swept its weight at 6M tokens with the fixed tool eval at n=200. Recall N32/N64: none 34/3, w0.1 62/25, w0.2 53/28, w0.3 56/22, w0.5 59/25, high-N-only (N16–32, w0.2) 66/28 — flat from 0.1 up. Tool skill (mode/tool/args/refuse) falls with weight: none .915/.893/.697/.923, w0.1 .885/.861/.680/.897, w0.2 .860/.844/.631/.846, w0.5 .865/.803/.574/.910. w0.1 keeps the whole recall gain for ~2–3 points. w0.05 and a second w0.2 seed were lost to the disk outage below.

The investigation shook out five bugs, all fixed and tested. (1) recall_eval --replay split is invalid on any stack with attention: pass 2 feeds only the query token and carries spiking state but no KV, so the v3 base “scored” 50/41/28/12/0 split vs 100/100/100/72/38 single-pass; split stays valid only for all-spiking 30M screens. (2) tool_eval/head_eval sliced the first N of a kind-grouped file, so every eval was 100% gold-ANSWER and tool_acc was 0/0 — tool calling had never been measured. (3) train_sft and train_dpo trained on the hash test split (2,454 tool items, 2,196 preference pairs). (4) grammar.TokenTable.mask cached on id(grammar); CPython reuses freed ids, so one tool set’s masks served another (FAIL 31/40 → PASS). (5) At 1B the checkpoint save died allocating pinned host memory; _to_cpu now falls back to a pageable copy.

v4 got its first hard throughput number. The 1B A′ shape (§66) on the 5090 at 72 seqs/step: B4 2.2k, B8 4.0k tok/s (28.2GB), B12 spills (12.7GB allocated, 33.7 reserved) — overhead-bound. At 4.0k, 16B tokens is ~46 days, so levers must deliver ≥1.55×. expandable_segments would fit B12 but fails under WSL (“device not ready”). The 30M depth screen closed NULL (L4 55.4 vs L2 55.8 val_ppl, same recall, 1.3–1.8× slower) → stay wide.

A fair transformer comparison: we win on samples, not on compute. A 30M, 2-layer transformer with the residual fix, same data/tokens/MQAR aux, lost at matched tokens (val_ppl 87.8 on the spiking-tuned NorMuon recipe, 68.8 on AdamW, vs spiking 55.8) and sat at chance on recall — until a 600M-token run showed a clean induction-head phase transition at ~200M tokens (loss 8.0→4.98). The spiking model learns recall inside 100M. But the transformer is ~6–8× cheaper per token: in 1.36h (less than spiking’s 1.5h for 100M) it reached val_ppl 39.7 and recall 100/97 vs spiking’s 55.8 and 59/38. At 30M and with unoptimized spiking kernels, the honest pitch is sample efficiency (and energy, unmeasured) — not quality per GPU-hour. The real test is a v3-matched 426M transformer (17L, 421.5M, AdamW 3e-4); it probed at ~10.8k tok/s on the 5080, i.e. ~10.7 days for 10B tokens.

Process lessons: two 426M SFT runs on the 5090 spill past 32GB (run arms sequentially), and sharing the 5080 between a screen and an eval OOM’d the screen. Then at 13:38 drive G:, which holds WSL’s ext4.vhdx, hit 0 free — every WSL process, down to sleep, segfaulted until Glenn freed space. See neuro_v3_sft_recall_loss_2026_09_26 + reference_wsl_g_drive_full + HANDOFF_2026_09_26.

Major version: 1.52 (v3 SFT erased recall [base 100/100/100/72/38 -> 62/69/22/0/0]; alpaca-only loses it too -> SFT itself, not the data mix. mqar_aug rehearsal in train_sft fixes it: recall flat w0.1-0.5, tool cost smallest at w0.1 [62/25; .885/.861/.680/.897 vs none .915/.893/.697/.923]. Bugs fixed: split-replay invalid on attention stacks; tool_eval slice all-ANSWER; SFT+ORPO test leakage; grammar mask cache id-reuse; 1B pinned-ckpt OOM. v4 1B A' B8 4.0k tok/s -> 16B ~46d; depth NULL. 30M transformer [residual fix]: spiking wins matched tokens [55.8 vs 68.8 AdamW], transformer wins matched compute [39.7, recall 100/97 in 1.36h] with an induction transition at ~200M tok; 426M v3-matched transformer probed 10.8k tok/s on 5080 [~10.7d/10B]. G: disk-full outage killed w0.05 + w0.2-seed1. See neuro_v3_sft_recall_loss_2026_09_26 + reference_wsl_g_drive_full + HANDOFF_2026_09_26.)

68. Chasing why a faster neuron “lost”, we find that every spiking model has been reading the next token — and the honest exact LIF wins on both speed and quality

2026-09-26 (evening) – 09-27

The rehearsal sweep (§67) finished: w0.05 matched w0.1’s tool skill with less recall (53/19), and a second w0.2 seed moved recall ~6 points and tool 3–5 points — as large as the gaps between arms. The one signal that held across seeds was refusal (.846 at w0.2 vs .897 at w0.1), so we chose W=0.1 and relaunched SFT→ORPO→OPD on the 5090.

Overnight on the 5080 we finally ran the profile the speed plan had asked for. temp/profile_train.py wraps the real train_gpu loop from outside (an optimizer-step hook drives torch.profiler), so no shared file changed under the live run. The v4 draft is launch-bound: the GPU is busy ~23–34% of a step that fires ~18.6k kernels, and a step costs ~770ms fixed plus ~54ms per sample — batch, i.e. VRAM, is the lever (B8 3,415 → B12 4,345–5,086 tok/s). muon_batched_ns (bit-identical) cut the optimizer step 280→181ms; block-level act_ckpt is bit-identical but collapses to 1,952 tok/s under whole-model compile; activation_memory_budget=0.5 let B16 fit for +8% in a fair back-to-back pair. Run-to-run noise from the 5090 job’s CPU load was ±15%, which retracted two early claims (a “+31%” no-sync gain and a “+39%” budget gain); Glenn asked for a clean 5090 throughput suite before any verdict is final.

The profile also showed that lif_parallel’s fixed-point loop never converges: every call hits the K=10 cap, and against a float64 sequential LIF it disagrees on 21–31% of spikes. The FPT paper’s contraction proof needs a strong leak (λ=0.25) and a sigmoid in the iterations; at β=0.9 with hard spikes the map is chaotic. We built src/lif_exact.py — a Triton kernel for the true sequential LIF, 0 spike flips and ~1e-6 gradient error vs the reference, 25–50× faster — and it “lost” a 30M screen 2× (185.7/159.7 vs 95.7/79.3 at 1k/2k steps). We then spent the day trying to rescue it: rate matching (target 0.18), no reset (γ=0), and cheaper FPT (K=1/3) all sat at 183–258; surrogate-gradient strength was equal across neurons; FPT was not memoryless. The odd one out was fpt_K: 0 — the same no-reset neuron as the kernel’s γ=0, in pure PyTorch — at 108 vs the kernel’s 195. The two matched exactly in fp32 and differed only in bf16.

The LIF scan leaked the future. _scan_chunked built its position index as torch.arange(L, dtype=x.dtype); under autocast that is bf16, which is exact only to 256 (257, 259, 261 → 256, 260, 260). Above 256 the causal decay mask was wrong and position t read x[t+1] — at layer 0, the embedding of the very token it is predicting. Changing one token at position 300 moved earlier logits by 13–17. Measured leaky vs honest val ppl: 30M control 48.2 vs 139.0; v3 flagship 16.6 vs 41.0 (positions <256 identical). Every bf16 spiking val_ppl in this doc since FPT training is inflated, including v1’s 19.61, v3’s 17.57 and the spiking-vs-transformer comparisons; MQAR recall (sequences <256) is not. It also fits the long-standing puzzle of strong val_ppl alongside chance-level downstream scores. Fix: fp32 index math (2e98665), verified equal to a leak-free reference with zero causal leakage. Post-training was paused mid-ORPO.

A leak-free re-evaluation of all 27 checkpoints (temp/reeval_leakfree.py) re-ranked the past screens: width, independent windows, block size 512 and staying wide all hold; gate_rank’s −16% quality win was the leak (181.5 vs 182.5 honest), though it still saves 23% FLOPs; v3 is 44.8 honest on the training eval windows. The best leak-trained 30M model is 2.3× worse than the transformer’s 68.5.

Trained honestly, the exact neuron wins on every axis. First leak-free 30M runs (seed 0, 100M tok): lif_exact val_ppl 99.3 vs fixed FPT 136.8 (−27%), single-pass recall N16/32/64 100/100/94% vs 97/62/19% — while its LIF runs 25–50× faster. The transformer is still ahead on ppl (68.5, gap 1.45×) but sits at chance on recall at this budget. Seed 1 of both arms is running.

We also priced the cloud for v4 (1B/16B) and the 426M transformer: H100 ~$1.5–3.5, H200 ~$3.6–4.6, B200 ~$3.75–6.8 per GPU-hour; a single H200/B200 fits 16B tokens in ~5–13 days for ~$500–1,300 (unmeasured — a 1-hour probe comes first), and credits large enough to matter need incorporation and weeks. A healing run for v3 (configs/heal_v3_363m.yaml, +1B tokens from the 10B checkpoint with the fix) is ready but on hold: if seed 1 confirms, v4 moves to lif_exact and the next question is closing the 1.45× ppl gap. See neuro_bf16_scan_future_leak_2026_09_27 + neuro_lif_fpt_nonconvergence_2026_09_27 + HANDOFF_2026_09_27.

Major version: 1.53 (FUTURE-TOKEN LEAK: lif_parallel._scan_chunked built arange in bf16 under autocast -> wrong causal mask above 256, position t reads x[t+1]; all bf16 spiking val_ppl inflated [v3 16.6 leaky vs 41.0 honest; 30M 48.2 vs 139.0]; recall unaffected; fixed 2e98665, post-training PAUSED. Leak-free re-eval of 27 ckpts: width/indepwin/bs512/stay-wide hold, gate_rank quality win was the leak [keep for FLOPs]. FPT never converges [always K=10, 21-31% spikes differ from true LIF]; exact-LIF Triton kernel src/lif_exact.py 25-50x faster; HONEST 30M: lif_exact 99.3 vs FPT 136.8 vs transformer 68.5, recall lif_exact 100/100/94 vs 97/62/19 -> likely v4 neuron, seed 1 running. Throughput: v4 draft launch-bound [~18.6k kernels/step], batch is the lever; muon_batched_ns bit-identical -35% optimizer step; act_ckpt loses under compile; activation_memory_budget +8%; 5080 numbers provisional pending a clean 5090 suite. SFT rehearsal W=0.1 chosen. Cloud priced; heal_v3 config ready, on hold. See neuro_bf16_scan_future_leak_2026_09_27 + neuro_lif_fpt_nonconvergence_2026_09_27 + HANDOFF_2026_09_27.)

69. Closing the honest gap: a hybrid layer and multi-level spikes bring us to 1.07× the transformer — then an interference test shows our “perfect recall” only held for immediate questions

2026-09-27 (evening) – 09-29

Glenn set the goal after §68: take the honest 30M gap (lif_exact 99.3 vs the 2-layer AdamW transformer 68.5, 1.45×) to ≤1.0×, ideally 0.9×, without giving up recall or sparsity. The first move was to measure where the gap lives. temp/diag_gap_positions.py splits validation loss by context position. The ratio was only 1.11× with 1–3 tokens of context, but 1.72× at positions 4–15 and 1.64× at 16–63, easing to 1.41× past 256. With the memory ablated, the curve went flat after position 3: every bit of our context use came from the GDN-2 memory. It is an induction-style store keyed on the previous token, and it has no sense of position. The weakness was precise use of recent context. It also explained why the old pre_conv (09-07) had been our best-perplexity screen while destroying recall: it added local mixing, but on the key/query pointer.

Our first lever was the wrong one. The transformer on our NorMuon recipe had scored 87.8, so we guessed the spiking recipe (tuned under the leak) was simply mis-tuned. It was not. NorMuon 0.02 was auto-killed; halving the LR cost 25% (103.1); AdamW matched perplexity (98.8) but drove recall to chance. The optimizer stays NorMuon 0.005 / AdamW 6e-4. The transformer’s own LR (1e-3) was confirmed as its best (5e-4 84.6, 2e-3 73.6), so the 68.5 baseline is fair.

Two structural wins that stack. We built seven default-off flags in one pass, each parity-proven byte-identical when off (temp/parity_gap_flags.py): ff_conv, swa_window, pre_norm, mem_out_gate, and three neuron options, lif_levels / lif_signed / lif_learn_beta (new lif_exact_ml kernel, 0 spike flips vs float64). We then ran them three at a time on the 5090.

  • A 2-layer hybrid [spk, attn] gave 82.5 (2 seeds).
  • Integer multi-level spikes (lif_levels: 4, I-LIF style: integer training, binary-spike inference) gave 85.0 (2 seeds), with pure spiking and recall 100×5.
  • Stacked with pre-norm and the output gate, the full stack reached 73.4 / 74.9 (seeds 0/1) = 1.07–1.09×, recall 100×5.
  • Canon-A (canon_a, Allen-Zhu’s residual short conv) added 71.9 on one seed.
The frequency split showed why the two wins stack. Attention fixed common tokens (1.06×), multi-level spikes fixed rare ones (1.13×).

Several levers came up null: ff_conv 99.2, the RoPE window read 106.3, learnable β 95.1, 8 levels 74.3 (vs 4 levels 73.4), and attention LR 0.01/0.02 (93.0/100.9). Depth hurt the comparison. A 3-layer [spk, attn, spk] + lev4 scored 69.3 against a param-matched 3-layer transformer at 59.4 (1.17×), because the 3-layer transformer forms induction heads within 100M tokens. Glenn asked whether a 1-attention-layer transformer would be fairer. We kept the 17-layer all-attention model as the 426M baseline, param-matched and standard, and noted a 1-attention-layer model as a future ablation. Its LR check (60M tokens) moved it to 6e-4 (93.4 vs 108.2 at 3e-4). The model runs at 29.9k tok/s, so 10B tokens is ~3.9 days on the 5090.

Our recall only held when the question came straight after the facts. temp/haystack_recall_eval.py puts 0–440 tokens of real text between the key–value block and the query. Plain lif_exact held N8 at 100/96/73/40% (gap 0/64/192/440), level with the 3-layer transformer’s 100/75/70/44. But the best-perplexity full stack fell to chance at a 64-token gap (100/8/10/15): the hybrid and multi-level levers had bought perplexity by giving up robustness. Our MQAR aux never has a gap. That is the “interference wall” (arXiv 2609.16183), whose fix is a distance curriculum. mqar_gap_max (haystack MQAR with a ramped real-text gap) did lift N8 to a flat ~70% at every gap, but at full strength it cost 25% perplexity (92.6) and immediate recall. On lif_exact it was plainly negative (121.6). A gentler dose is next, and every headline recall number now carries the haystack column.

Two framing pieces came out of the same days. docs/transformer_comparison_scorecard.md lists 25 features in six groups with honest status. Today we measurably win on sparsity, and brains are a unique capability not yet measured. We currently lose on quality, recall under interference, training speed, and post-training stability. Alignment & Safety became its own HIGH-priority group after this month’s AI-escape incidents. The first targets are interpretability (monosemanticity of spike neurons vs transformer MLP neurons) and alignment-training response. Those two words enter marketing only after matched, measured wins. Meanwhile v3’s heal started on the 5090 (09-28 15:24, resumed at 10B). It is healing steadily: val_ppl 44.8 → 41.4 by 10.58B. It chains into post-training on the healed base for Glenn’s demo.

Major version: 1.54 (Honest gap diagnosed: loss ratio by context position 1.11x @1-3, 1.72x @4-15, 1.41x @256+ -> weak precise recent-context use. LR hypothesis wrong [0.02 killed, half LR 103, AdamW 98.8 but recall at chance]. Wins, parity-proven default-off flags: hybrid [spk,attn] 82.5 [2 seeds]; lif_levels 4 85.0 [2 seeds]; full stack hybrid+lev4+pre_norm+mem_out_gate 73.4/74.9 = 1.07-1.09x tfmr; canon_a 71.9 [1 seed]. Nulls: ff_conv, swa read, learn_beta, lev8, attn LR. 3L: 69.3 vs 3L tfmr 59.4 [1.17x]. Haystack recall eval: full stack at chance with a 64-tok gap [lif_exact ~ 3L tfmr]; mqar_gap_max curriculum fixes interference [N8 ~70% flat] but costs +25% ppl at full strength -> gentler dose next. 426M tfmr: 17L kept, LR 6e-4, 29.9k tok/s. v3 heal running [44.8 -> 41.4]. Scorecard with Alignment & Safety HIGH. See neuro_ppl_gap_diagnosis_2026_09_27 + docs/ppl_gap_plan_2026_09_27.md + docs/transformer_comparison_scorecard.md + HANDOFF_2026_09_29.)

70. Signed spikes beat the transformer on two seeds, the interference failure turns out to be a memory that stopped remembering — and our screens were running at half speed all along

2026-09-29 – 09-30

We started from the open wound of §69: the full stack answered immediate questions perfectly and fell to chance with 64 tokens of text in between. The obvious suspect was the attention layer answering the synthetic questions and leaving the memory idle. temp/diag_haystack_pathways.py ablated each pathway and killed that idea. Switching attention off changed nothing, and switching the GDN memory off dropped every model to chance. Recall is entirely the memory, in every model. What differed was how long the memory held anything. On real text, the median decay half-life per memory channel was 47–76 tokens for plain lif_exact, but 0–1 token for the hybrid, the full stack, Canon-A and the spiking MLP. With attention covering local context, the LM loss had simply taught the memory to wipe itself every step.

Signed multi-level spikes: 0.89× the transformer, recall restored, two seeds. lif_signed on the full stack scored 61.36 / 61.11 (mean 61.24 vs the 2-layer transformer’s 68.5). Recall held at 100×5, and the haystack came back to N8 88–90 / 63–65 / 34–35% (the full stack had 8/10/15). The memory half-life went back up to 97–113 / 55–62 tokens. Adding Canon-A made it 59.74 (1 seed), level with the 3-layer transformer (59.38) using two layers. We do not know why signed spikes restore the horizon. The obvious hypothesis was tested and rejected: unsigned codes giving memory keys a shared direction. Key overlap was highest in lif_exact, the model with the best gapped recall.

Two follow-ups tempered it. A 3-layer signed [spk, attn, spk] scored 64.65 with its memory half-life back at ~2 tokens and the haystack at chance. The long horizon is a fragile learned equilibrium, not a property of signed spikes, which matters for the 9-layer v4. The gentler gap curriculum failed too. With gaps capped at 128, the MQAR aux never clicked in 100M tokens (total loss stuck near 9 against 4.3 without gaps). The 440-gap run of §69 had only clicked after step 4000. So gaps delay recall acquisition; if we use them again, they should come after recall is learned.

Glenn asked whether a stateful model should be trained the way it lives: in a continuous stream instead of on random windows that start with amnesia. The June attempt (§10) had been negative on a Hebbian memory. This time the case looked stronger. Carrying state at eval already gave random-trained models −3 to −7% ppl, and the 440-gap model got 24% worse with carried state. That is the “unexplored states” failure of Buitrago & Gu (ICML 2025), for which state passing is the published fix. We scoped it (docs/contiguous_training_design.md), pre-registered the criteria, and fixed the stale June path (it had silently skipped the MQAR aux). We also added a SWAX-style stochastic attention window.

Contiguous training: closed negative at 30M. The two stateful runs matched each other (70.3 / 70.5 zero-state, streaming 65.3 / 65.2 vs the signed control’s 64.3). They gained a little haystack at medium gaps and nothing at 440. Over one-million-token streams the signed random-trained models were already perfectly stable. Only the 440-gap model exploded (memory norm 27.7→75.5), exactly the case state passing exists for. The stochastic window changed nothing in training. But it showed that a 64-token attention window at inference costs only ~1% ppl on signed models, so constant-memory inference is viable.

The screens had been running at half speed. The stateful runs trained at 30–35k tok/s against 14–15k for everything else. Tracing it: the WSL virtual disk reports itself rotational with an 8 MB read-ahead. Every cold 2 KB random window read pulled 8 MB (~37 ms), and a 32-window batch took ~1.2 s, about the whole step. The drive is a SATA SSD, and sorting or threading the reads did not help. MADV_RANDOM on a separate memory map for the random-window path took a batch from 1220 ms to 28 ms with byte-identical data. Screens now run ~2× faster, and the v4 draft (72 random windows per step) should gain ~1.3–1.4×.

Two accounting corrections closed the session. The [spike] spiking_only log line rescaled a rate that spike_rate_undiluted had already made spiking-only, so every logged rate was doubled. Measured directly, signed spikes fire on 12.7% of (neuron, token) slots, not 26% (lam 2.0: 6.7%; full stack 4.2%). energy.py had the same double count. The energy model itself then set expectations. At 512 tokens the LM head is 47% of per-token energy, the dense attention block 35%, and spikes only 1%, so sparsity alone cannot deliver a 90% energy claim. The real wins are elsewhere. Matching the 3-layer transformer’s quality with 2 layers costs 1.6× less energy per token. With a 64-token window, per-token energy is constant in context length: 7.3× less than the quality-matched transformer at 32k tokens and 24× at 128k. Sparsity screens toward 10% and 5% are queued.

Major version: 1.55 (Haystack failure root-caused: recall = GDN memory in every model; hybrid/full/canon memory half-life 0-1 tok vs lif_exact 47-76. SIGNED spikes 61.36/61.11 = 0.89x tfmr, haystack + horizon restored; signed+canon 59.74 = 3L tfmr with 2 layers; signed 3L collapses [half-life ~2] -> fragile equilibrium. gap128 failed [gaps delay MQAR acquisition]. Contiguous training CLOSED-NEG [r9a/b/c; 1M-token streams stable without it; only gap440 explodes]; SWA-64 inference ~1% cost. 30M screens were 2x data-bound [8 MB read-ahead] -> MADV_RANDOM fix deployed. Spike log + energy.py double-counted: true firing 12.7%; spikes = 1% of energy; real energy wins = quality-matched 1.6x + long-context 7-24x with SWA. See docs/contiguous_training_design.md + neuro_ppl_gap_diagnosis_2026_09_27 + HANDOFF_2026_09_30.)

71. A gentle dose of gap training fixes long-range recall for free, a third layer exposes a depth problem — and throughput scoping finds the fused neuron already built

2026-09-30 (Neuro 46)

The gentle gap curriculum works. §69 fixed haystack interference with gap-MQAR at full weight but paid +25% ppl. At mqar_weight 0.15 (screen_r7_gapw015, unsigned full stack, gap 440) val_ppl was 71.10, slightly better than the ~74.9 control. Haystack recall was 99–100% at every gap, N8 and N32, and the memory half-life on text went from ~1 token to ~3,700. The memory learned to remember without the LM paying for it. r12 puts the same dose on the best stack.

That best stack is now confirmed. signed+canon seed 1 gave 59.29 against seed 0’s 59.74: a two-seed mean of 59.52, 2.8% better than signed alone and about 0.87× the 2-layer transformer, with recall and horizon intact at 12.5% firing. The sparsity sweep mapped the signed stack’s Pareto curve. λ 0.5/1/2/4 gave 61.24/62.73/64.60/67.46 at true firing 12.7/9.3/6.7/4.8%: about +3% ppl per 30% cut in firing, and recall survives at every point.

Depth works against us. Glenn asked whether a third layer is worth some ppl. Going 2L→3L, the transformer improved 14% (68.8→59.38) and the unsigned stack 11%, but the signed stack got worse (61.24→63.35/64.65). So the 0.89× lead holds at two layers but becomes ~1.08× at three, and v4 plans nine. The signed-vs-unsigned gain also shrinks from 18% to 4%, so signed spikes and depth look like substitutes. The 3L horizon collapse of §70 proved seed-dependent (half-life 2 vs 68 tokens). A third spiking layer also costs +32% FLOPs but 1.7× wall time. Depth screens r13a ([spk, attn, attn]), r13b (depth_scaled_init) and r13d (2L at 4,096 neurons, param-matched) are queued, to be judged at matched wall-clock.

The throughput question then turned into two scoping passes. First, the “fused neuron kernel” already exists: lif_exact_ml, one Triton forward and one backward kernel, used by every current 30M screen. The 10-iteration FPT still lives only in the v4 draft and the 1B probe. Kernel counts made the point: one FPT call is 277 kernels plus 10 host syncs, the fused neuron 12 plus 0. The compiled census at the 1B-probe structure went from 7,857 to 5,432 kernels per fwd+bwd (−31%), with all 80 syncs gone. Most of what remains is the GDN-2 chunk loop, which also explains the 1.7× 3L wall time. We first under-estimated the neuron’s share by counting eager launches; the compiled count corrected that, and both docs say so.

Second, CUDA graphs. The old crash (neuro_cudagraphs_incompat_2026_08_09) has a deeper cause than MQAR. Inductor’s cudagraph trees keep gradients inside the graph pool, so gradient accumulation across replays is unsupported (pytorch#169545; rf-detr turns graphs off for GA>1), and v4 accumulates over 9 micro-batches. The plan is Megatron/TE-style per-spiking-block torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables, gated on the GPU still being ≤60% busy after the neuron switch. A 1B FPT-vs-lif_exact_ml probe will settle that on the 5090 once Neuro 44’s post-training chain exits. Glenn also asked about multi-head memory. The July cliff (recall 91→11% at 8 heads) was measured on the old stack, so a re-test is logged as a low-priority idea, since 16 heads would unlock fused GDN-2 kernels. Finally, the 5080 scheduler now waits for the GPU to be idle for 5 minutes before every start, because Glenn sometimes plays past midnight.

Pointers: docs/fused_neuron_kernel_design.md, docs/cuda_graphs_v4_design.md, the 2026-09-30 entries in docs/to-do.html, HANDOFF_2026_09_30b.md.

Major version: 1.56 (Gentle gap curriculum WORKS: mqar_weight 0.15 + gap 440 = 71.10 [no ppl cost], haystack ~100% at all gaps, half-life ~3700 tok; r12 = signed+canon + it. signed+canon 2-seed 59.52 [0.87x tfmr] = best. Signed sparsity Pareto lambda 0.5/1/2/4 = 61.24/62.73/64.60/67.46 @ 12.7/9.3/6.7/4.8%. DEPTH WARNING: 2L->3L tfmr -14%, unsigned -11%, signed +3-5% -> lead may vanish at v4 depth; 3L 1.7x wall time; r13a/b/d queued. Throughput: fused neuron already exists [lif_exact_ml]; compiled census 7,857->5,432 kernels [-31%], 80->0 syncs; GDN-2 chunk loop dominates. CUDA graphs: trees blocked by grad accumulation -> per-block make_graphed_callables, gated on GPU-busy ≤60% after the neuron switch; 1B probe A/B queued on the 5090. 5080 scheduler waits for a free GPU. See docs/fused_neuron_kernel_design.md + docs/cuda_graphs_v4_design.md + HANDOFF_2026_09_30b.)

72. Depth comes from attention, not more spiking layers — then Glenn asks for a model he can audit, and the review finds our Engram has been half-blind

2026-10-01 (Neuro 47)

The night started with a silent failure. The 5080 scheduler, the 1B probe waiter and a watchdog all died at 00:41. They had been launched as background tasks of the previous Claude session, and they ended when it closed. We relaunched them with setsid -f, which survives the session (memory: reference_wsl_background_launch).

Round 11 settled sparsity. A late ramp beats a hinge, and both beat the constant-λ Pareto curve of §71:

  • r11c (ramp to 10%) gave 60.19 at 9.5% firing, only +0.75% over signed+canon.
  • r11b (ramp to 5%) gave 60.87 at 6.7%, where the λ curve gives 64.60.

The gentle gap dose turned out not to be free on signed stacks. r12 (2L) gave 67.32 (+12.7%) and r14c (3L) gave 57.57 (+15%) at twice the wall time, although haystack was 99–100% in both.

The depth warning had an answer: add attention, not spiking layers.

  • r13a [spk, attn, attn] gave 51.24, confirmed by r14a on seed 1 at 51.44. That is 0.86× the 3L transformer, at the speed of the 2L stack.
  • With canon and the late ramp, r14b reached 50.14.
  • At 4L, r14d [spk, attn×3] reached 45.41, though haystack weakened.
  • A second spiking layer stayed worse: r13b gave 63.14, and depth_scaled_init made no difference.

That success raised a problem. Glenn pointed out that two thirds of our best stack is now ordinary transformer layers. Safety and alignment are the field’s main concern, and spiking is our differentiation, so the model must be interpretable and auditable, stay spiking, and still win.

The research reframed the question. Attention is the most auditable part of a transformer, because it records what was read. Top-k or winner-take-all attention is cheap and is counted as neuromorphic in the 2026 literature. The real loss of interpretability was the dense MLPs: each “attention” layer turned out to be two thirds GELU MLP.

A first CPU measurement was humbling. At matched activity, our spiking neurons are no more token-selective than the transformer’s MLP units: median top-1 share 0.093 vs 0.128 (temp/diag_neuron_selectivity.py). Spiking does not make a model interpretable by itself. Width, lateral inhibition and a measured scorecard have to design it in (docs/interpretable_spiking_plan_2026_10_01.md).

We suspected a plumbing cause for the depth problem. A spiking block adds LayerNorm(r+ff) to the residual at unit scale, so it cannot start near-identity, and that norm also undoes depth_scaled_init. Round 15 built four default-off, bit-identical flags: attn_ffn, a k-winner-take-all SpikeGLU, attn_topk and spk_layerscale.

That hypothesis was only half right. r15d showed that LayerScale cures the slow start (eval-1 147 vs 191) but not the final deficit (64.29 vs 64.65/63.35). A second spiking layer is genuinely weaker than attention plus an MLP. The remaining suspects are interfering memories, or a spiking MLP that is too narrow and too smoothed over time.

The code review found a real bug. EngramV2 keeps the low bits of a linear hash. With K = 8192, token ids x and x±8192 share a bucket in every table, measured at 100% (temp/check_engram_hash.py). Every 30M screen merged tokens 2 ways. The live v3 flagship and the v4 draft (32k vocab) merged every token 4 ways.

The review (docs/code_review_interpretability_2026_10_01.md) also found:

  • The model cannot stream token by token exactly: the LIF membrane, canon taps and Engram history all reset on every call.
  • Only about 3% of its operations are event-driven.
  • Multi-level spikes are effectively ternary.
  • Our transformer baselines lacked independent windows and Engram, so our lead is overstated.
  • The v4 draft is obsolete.

The new v4 proposal (docs/v4_recipe_research_2026_10_01.md) is a transformer-shaped stack:

  • every MLP is a spiking neuron layer;
  • mixing is top-k gated attention;
  • the memory sits in 2–3 layers;
  • Engram uses the fixed hash.

Before the 30-day run it passes five gates: r15, r16, a mid-scale check, a 1B probe, then launch.

Pointers: the 2026-10-01 entries in docs/to-do.html; memory neuro_interpretable_spiking_plan_2026_10_01; temp/mk_gap_r15_configs.py; temp/parity_r15_flags.py; HANDOFF_2026_10_01.md.

Major version: 1.57 (Depth via attention: r13a/r14a [spk,attn,attn] 51.24/51.44, r14b +canon+ramp10 = 50.14, r14d 4L = 45.41. Late sparsity ramp wins (r11c 60.19 @ 9.5%). Gap w0.15 costs 13-15% on signed stacks. Glenn's pivot to interpretable + auditable + spiking. Measured: spiking neurons no more selective than MLP units. r15 flags built (attn_ffn, SpikeGLU, attn_topk, spk_layerscale); r15d: LayerScale fixes only the slow start. Engram low-bit hash BUG (v3/v4 4-way token merge). Code review + new v4 proposal. Scheduler relaunched with setsid after a session-kill.)

73. The v4 candidate beats every 4-layer model we have, the spiking MLP turns out to cost 9% for circuits 7× smaller — and the slowness is all in the first layer

2026-10-02 (Neuro 48)

Round 15 changed the plan before it finished. Taking the MLP out of the attention layers cost 27% (r15a 63.77), and moving those parameters into a wider spiking layer recovered almost nothing (r15g 61.62). A k-winner-take-all SpikeGLU that was sparse from step 0 cost 7.9% (r15b 54.10), while top-k attention was free (r15c 50.52). The fair 3-layer transformer (r15e 53.67) cut our honest margin from the quoted 14–16% to 6.6%, and Engram with the old hash added nothing (r15f 49.80). A four-iteration review rebuilt the v4 plan around a stronger spiking MLP: a dendritic layer (DSN) that runs dense, then sparsifies late (docs/v4_plan_review_2026_10_01.md).

Round 16, the v4 candidate, in a leave-one-out design (4 layers).

  • c0 [spiking memory layer, (gated top-k attention + DSN)×3] scored 43.75 / 43.47 on two seeds, against 45.41 for the old 4-layer reference r14d.
  • The same model with GELU MLPs (l3) scored 40.13. So the other changes together are worth −11.6%, and the DSN costs 9%. That cost appears only while the k-ramp sparsifies.
  • For that 9%, the DSN gives circuits 7.6× smaller (43k vs 331k attribution entries), more selective units and 23% fewer ops per token.
  • The LIF-gated DSN (x2) scored 44.36 and had the best haystack of the round.
  • The fair 4-layer transformer (t4) scored 48.05. But it trains 2.6× faster, so our win holds only at matched tokens.

Overnight we built three tools.

  • An interpretability scorecard (scripts/interp_scorecard.py). Its headline: 93–100% of correct recalls trace back to the single memory write that stored the fact.
  • A knowledge-graph recall eval (scripts/kg_recall_eval.py).
  • A 23.15B-token corpus, about half of it FineWeb-Edu (scripts/build_corpus_v4.py). Two silent slowdowns had to be fixed along the way: WSL networking ran 4× slower than Windows, and np.isin took 18 s per batch.

Both our model and the transformer have an in-context reversal curse. Asked a value, neither can name its key: reverse recall was 0–8%, below chance. Glenn's video study (the brain, YOCO, Merlin-Arthur, decision engines) turned this into an r17 proposal for a bidirectional, many-reader hippocampus (docs/v4_brain_principles_review_2026_10_02.md).

We also profiled the 30M shape on the 5090.

  • The transformer runs at 113k tok/s; c0, x2 and l3 run at 31–32k.
  • Neither the DSN nor top-k attention is the bottleneck. Flash attention instead of top-k gains only +5%.
  • The memory layer is the bottleneck. Chunk 256 without checkpointing gains +26% with exact math. Batch 64 gains +68%, which means the run is launch-bound.
  • Whole-model torch.compile collapses to 791 tok/s on c0, while it speeds the transformer up to 190k.

We also stopped the 5090's head_conf stage. Its on-policy labels were all one class, so it had spent 14 hours learning a constant.

Major version: 1.58 (Round 16: c0 43.61 [2 seeds] vs r14d 45.41; DSN costs 9% vs GELU l3 40.13 for 7.6x smaller circuits; t4 48.05 but 2.6-3.6x faster. Scorecard, KG recall eval, 23.15B corpus built. In-context reversal curse in both models. 30M profile: the H0 layer is the cost; whole-model compile collapses c0 to 791 tok/s. See HANDOFF_2026_10_02.)

74. The speed plan: a recompile storm fixed, and at 1B the gap to the transformer shrinks to 1.61× once the optimizer is amortized

2026-10-02 (afternoon) – 10-03 (Neuro 48)

The op profiles first raised a doubt: c0 ran NorMuon unbatched while t4 ran fused AdamW. A clean timing split on a free 5090 (a pause-the-queue protocol Glenn asked us to keep) settled it at 30M: NorMuon is ~15% of the gap, the memory layer ~80%. Chunk 256 without chunk checkpointing gives +32% with exact math, and Glenn made it standard from r18 together with telemetry_eval_only and batched NS.

Compile had been failing for a simple reason: every MQAR length recompiled, because GDN-2's chunk loop unrolls on T (530 recompiles in 7 steps), and the DSN k-ramp would have recompiled every step too. mqar_pad_to right-pads MQAR to one length (every layer is causal, so the query logit is unchanged to 1e-5), and the k-WTA moved into a compiler-disabled _kwta helper. Compiled c0 went to 60.2k tok/s, 1.95× the original.

G4, the 1B probe. c0-S1 (1.109B: H0 + 12×[attention + DSN 4d]) against a param-matched transformer (1.096B) at B8: 5,960 vs 15,251 tok/s, a 2.56× gap. But NorMuon's Newton–Schulz took ~460 of c0's ~690 ms per step. With grad accumulation 8 it amortizes. A first GA8 pair read 1.33×, but both runs sat at the WDDM spill point; with expandable_segments the clean numbers are 10,391 vs 16,746 tok/s, a 1.61× gap, down from 3.6× at the start of the day.

Both models keep the GPU ~75–80% busy at 1B, so CUDA graphs can buy at most ~1.25×. Glenn wants them anyway, since they are exact. A prototype (temp/proto_graphs.py) gives 1.22× over per-block compile at 30M with bit-identical gradients; the k-ramp still has to be made graph-safe. A B4 compile crash turned out to be an Inductor SplitScan codegen bug on the GDN-2 cumsum; B6 and above compile fine.

Round 17 tuned the DSN on one seed each: k 688 (r17f) 41.42, k 512 + ramp from 40% 41.77, hidden 4096 42.16, ramp from 30% 42.69. The LIF-gated DSN with a 20% sparsity target (r17j) scored only 44.28 but had the best haystack of rounds 16–17 (N32 at gap 440: 13.3 vs ~7). Seed repeats and an f+g combination run overnight.

Major version: 1.59 (Speed plan: 30M split H0 ~80% / NorMuon ~15%; std flags from r18 [chunk256, no chunk ckpt, telemetry_eval_only, batched NS, compile + mqar_pad_to]; compile fix 1.95x; G4 1B gap 2.56x at GA1 -> 1.61x at GA8 [expandable_segments; 1.33x was a spill artefact]; GPU busy 75-80%; CUDA graphs prototype 1.22x bit-identical; B4 Inductor SplitScan bug. r17: f 41.42, i 41.77, h 42.16, g 42.69, j 44.28 [best haystack]. See docs/speed_plan_v4_2026_10_02.md + neuro_v4_speed_plan_2026_10_02 + HANDOFF_2026_10_03.)

75. A sparse MLP beats the spiking one on every axis — then at 150M the hippocampus goes silent, because attention can already reach everything

2026-10-03 – 10-04 (Neuro 49)

The overnight seed repeats settled the DSN question of §74: r17k (k 688 + ramp from 30%) averaged 41.17 over three seeds, +2.2% against the GELU MLP of l3 (40.27), with haystack ~25% weaker in every DSN variant. GELU won on quality but its circuits were ~5× larger. Glenn asked whether GELU could get the three factors (sparsity, interpretability, energy) instead.

Round 19 swapped GELU for relu² (Primer) with an optional homeostatic threshold (SparseAct, attn_ffn_homeo): a neuron fires only above a threshold that is not trained by gradient but nudged after each step so the firing rate tracks a target that ramps from 1.0 to the goal between 60% and 90% of training. LIF membranes stay in the hippocampal layer only.

Sparsity turns out to be free. Three seeds each: per-layer threshold at 10% active 39.88, per-neuron 10% 40.09, 5% 40.35, all at or below GELU's 40.27, with GELU-level haystack. At 5% the LM circuits are 33.7k entries against GELU's 330.6k (9.8× smaller) and the units are the most selective of any arm. It also trains 9% faster than DSN at 1B. Glenn chose 5%: interpretability matters more to him than 1% perplexity.

Speed work in parallel. compile_regional had compiled nothing (the LM calls forward_sequence, not forward); fixed, it is still slower than whole-model compile. The MQAR aux pass turned out to be ~32% of the 1B step (16.7k tok/s with it off vs 11.3k on). mqar_once_per_step is quality-neutral at 30M (39.81 vs 39.96) but at 1B it exceeds the 5090's 32 GB; it is meant for the H200.

G3: at 150M the hippocampus is never used. At 12 layers and 1B tokens the candidate (H0 + 11×[attention + sparse MLP]) scored 27.26 against the fair transformer's 26.71 and ran ~1.8× slower. Worse, its memory-Δ was ≤0 from the first eval, the write gate sat 97% at its floor, and the spike rate fell from 0.28 to 0.004, while recall stayed 100%. At 30M, recall had been 100% memory. Haystack was equally poor in both models (~20% at N8).

Two things explain it. First, haystack is a training problem: the late gap dose x1 gives 100% at every gap at 30M, with the sparse MLP too (r22a, +1.7%). Second, the memory only works when attention cannot reach. A fixed 128-token window (attn_window_fixed) gives 100% beyond-window recall through memory at 30M (r22b, +2.9%). But at 150M it does nothing for the memory, because 11 stacked windows reach ~11×128 tokens, more than the 512-token context. SWAX (arXiv 2509.24552) reports the same: memory learns only when attention is short-sighted, with 1:1 memory:attention layers and 16k training sequences. Round 21 now tests a 32-token window and the gap dose from step 0.

The deadline is now concrete: Glenn wants to demo v4 to his son by October 19. Pretraining will run on an interruptible Vast H200 (~$0.44/h), so the config has to freeze by about October 8.

Major version: 1.60 (r17 seeds: DSN r17k 41.17 vs GELU 40.27. r19 sparse MLP [relu2 + homeostatic threshold] 39.88 at 10% / 40.35 at 5% = GELU quality, circuits 8-10x smaller; Glenn picks 5%. Regional compile fixed but not a lever; MQAR aux = 32% of the 1B step; mqar_once_per_step quality-neutral. G3 150M: hippocampus unused at depth [beta collapse, spikes 0.004]; x1 = haystack 100% at 30M; window 128 forces memory at 30M but not at 12 layers [reach ~L x W]; SWAX. Demo Oct 19, Vast H200. See neuro_g3_hippocampus_unused_2026_10_04 + HANDOFF_2026_10_04.)

76. The H200 is twice as fast as we needed, the memory writes but nobody reads it, and the demo page gets built

2026-10-04 evening (Neuro 50)

Glenn opened the Vast account and we rented an H200 NVL for an hour. His DSL uploads at 1.3 Mbps, so the 46 GB corpus would take ~80 hours: we benchmarked on a 16 MB slice and will build the corpus on the instance itself (scripts/build_corpus_v4.py, ≥300 GB disk). The setup script was missing snntorch and friends; fixed.

38.4k tok/s for the 1B shape. configs/v4_cloud_bench.yaml (r19d shape, 5% MLP, x1 gap MQAR on every step as the worst case): B64×GA1 38.4k, B32×GA2 34.0k, with mqar_once_per_step 35.0k, MQAR off 42.3k. 16B tokens in ~4.8 days, against the 18.5k tok/s we needed for 10. The MQAR aux is 19% of the step here, not the 32% of the 5090.

Glenn's question set the agenda for the rest of the evening: the demo must show the memory, not lean on attention. Four runs narrowed down why the hippocampus of §75 is silent at 12 layers:

  • r21c (window 128 + late x1): memory-Δ 0.00 while only 28% of the write gate sits at its floor. The memory writes; the layers above ignore what it says. Haystack beyond the window is at chance (N8 14%), ppl 40.82 (+12%).
  • r21g (window 32 + x1 from step 0, so 11×32 < the 440-token gap): ppl 48.45 (+33%), recall 88/81/78/25/9, but haystack N8 57% at gap 440. When attention truly cannot relay, the memory partly learns.
  • r21i (full window + x1 from step 0): haystack 100% at every gap, N8 and N32, memory-Δ +0.10, ppl 43.32 (+19%). Attention learns the in-context haystack itself when trained on it.
  • r23a (G3 width, only 4 layers, 5080): memory-Δ +6.9 at the end (~9% of ppl). Depth is a cause.

So inside the context, attention does the memory's job once it is trained to. The memory's only unique job is beyond the context. Two "both learn" recipes are queued on the 5090: r24a (SWAX stochastic window 32/full) and r24b (stateful 512-token segments, memory carried, reset 10%), each = r21i + one change (temp/mk_r24_configs.py). The transformer baselines r21d/e/f were dropped.

The demo page went from design (docs/demo_brain_view_design.md) to stage 1 in one sitting: scripts/demo/server.py + index.html, a brain dot-grid, spike raster, MLP and memory-write strips, a memory-off replay and an attention-window selector, with no change to src/. The energy meter counts every multiply-add per word and shows 1.38× fewer operations on the 30M model, the honest number: the relu² up-projection stays dense, so 10× would have been a lie.

The memory has never been taught a sentence. In the demo, "Anna's secret password is blue tiger" followed by filler fails beyond a 32-token attention window even with memory on. All the recall training uses synthetic MQAR token ranges. "Tell it a fact, ask later" needs natural-text long-range training, which segment training provides.

Glenn then asked for longer attention and memory. The plan is two tiers: attention over a 1–2k context added in a late long-context phase, memory for everything beyond it, trained by segments. Top-k attention builds full T×T scores, so this needs a kernel first; it is queued behind r24.

Major version: 1.61 (H200 NVL bench 38.4k tok/s at 1B [16B tok ~4.8 d]; corpus built on-instance due to 1.3 Mbps upload. Memory at depth: writes but ignored [r21c delta 0]; w32 forces partial learning [r21g haystack 57%, +33% ppl]; full window + early x1 = attention-only haystack 100% [r21i, +19%]; 4L uses memory [r23a]. r24a SWAX / r24b stateful queued. Demo stage 1 built [honest 1.38x ops]; natural-language recall beyond window fails. See neuro_g3_hippocampus_unused_2026_10_04 + HANDOFF_2026_10_05.)
